# Manuscript movement review

Project root: /Users/drive/fractured-path-monroe13
This is a compiled prompt, not evidence that a model ran or chapters were written.

---

## REVIEW

# O'Connor movement review

Read the complete supplied movement in chapter order before judging it. State
whether this is self-review, a fresh-context simulated cold read, or a real human
read. Do not claim independent testing when you have the drafting history.

First give an unscored reader response: where interest caught, where it slipped,
which person matters, which event you expect next, and whether you would continue.
Then use two perspectives: a fluent thirteen-year-old reader and an adult genre
reader. These are lenses, not demographic research. Do not infer a sales forecast.

For each applicable dimension give a 1–10 score, an exact passage location, a reason
and confidence. Mark an absent feature N/A instead of giving invented evidence:

| Dimension | What to judge |
|---|---|
| Opening pull | A concrete reason to care before explanation takes over |
| Keep reading | Unresolved pressure and satisfying local payoff |
| Interest / freshness | Specific people, choices and problems |
| Clarity / flow | Meaning on one pass and clean transitions |
| Character attachment | Agency, vulnerability, recognizable differences |
| Humor / warmth | Character-grounded pleasure, timing, emotional honesty |
| Action / suspense | Trackable contest, adaptation, cost, earned scene length |
| Progression payoff | Effort visibly changes capability and consequence |
| Connection | State, plants and relationships carried across chapters |
| Read-aloud quality | Punctuation, referents, dialogue attribution and cadence |

Anchor interpretation: 5 = understandable but inconsistent; 7 = engaging with
located weaknesses; 9 = compelling with few substantial distractions. These are
editorial judgments, not calibrated measurements. Do not average the two readers
into a release gate. Do not award points for matching another book's word counts.

Separately check canon, physical state, power limits, knowledge boundaries and
reserved disclosures. Cite the project evidence for a contradiction. If only the
plan supports a claim, identify it as plan adherence rather than a canon error.

Return a concise repair brief with at most three priorities. Each needs location,
observed issue, effect on the reader, proposed scope and a strength to preserve.
Retain the developed action the owner requested. Punctuation can repair an unclear
sentence; do not shorten a successful fight merely to reduce its word count.

If comparing runs, keep chapter coverage and scoring rubric equal. Hide model and
seat labels from a fresh reader where feasible. Report tradeoffs and uncertainty;
one movement does not prove which seat is better across a series.

---

## REVIEW COVERAGE

9 files supplied in declared order. Only claim this coverage. If the movement is incomplete, say so. Editorial canon evidence is present only when supplied below. Without it, report apparent inconsistency, not verified canon violations.

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-02-iron-circuit/manuscript/chapter-52.md

# Chapter 52 — Six Hundred

Dace was at the main door with his little book open in one hand and the other hand flat on a man's chest.

"No," he was saying. "You were told Thursday. I wrote you down Thursday, and then I drew a line through you, because you told me you were bringing your wife's brothers. Your wife's brothers are four of the biggest men on this hill." He did not raise his voice. "Stand in the street. The doors are open. You'll see the floor from the step if you stand on your toes, and if you can't see it you'll hear it. And if you push past me you'll not come in this building again while I'm alive."

The man looked at the hand on his chest, and then at Dace, and went back out into the street with his wife's brothers behind him, grumbling, to find a place on the step.

Cael came up the last of the stairs from the side passage with his coat over his arm. Dace saw him without turning his head.

"Five hundred and four," said Dace, to the book. "Inside. On the stone, on the benches, on the ladders and the beams, and two on the slate shelf that I'll have down before the first exchange if I have to pull them down by the ankles." He made a mark. "And the street, which I can't count, because it keeps moving. Call it a hundred. Call it more. The doorkeeper says it goes back to the pump." He shut the book on his pencil. "Six hundred people, near enough, and not one of them paid what it's worth, and I'll be sweeping them out of my corners till Thursday." He looked at Cael at last. "Go on. You're the only one I don't have to count."

The building was hot. That was the first thing.

The night before, with four hundred in it, the old foundry had been warm the way a kitchen is warm. Tonight the air under the low ceiling was thick and close, and it smelled of lamp oil and wet wool and too many people breathing the same breath. Dace had found more lamps again, from somewhere. They hung from every beam and every nail, so close together now that the ceiling was one sheet of small yellow flames, and their heat came down on the crowd while the crowd's heat went up to meet it. At Cael's back, through the great open doors, the cold of the street came in long draughts that touched his neck and were gone.

He went along the wall toward the north end, and the room saw him going.

It was not a cheer. Six hundred people cannot cheer for a boy walking along a wall; they did not know yet what they would be cheering for. It was more like a change in the weather of the noise. Heads turned down the room ahead of him in a long ripple, and voices dropped where he passed and rose again behind him with his name in them. He heard *that's him*, and *he's younger than I thought*, and *the one with no Path*, and once, from somebody on the beams overhead, *small, isn't he*, which made the people round the speaker laugh. He did not look up.

The eel-market men had their two pots going in the corner by the slate, with every bowl out and in use. The betting man had given up his trestle board entirely and was standing on it instead, calling the two columns over the crowd in a voice that had gone hoarse an hour ago. The mending-stall pennants hung over the side door, a dozen red triangles with a white rope stitched on each, and somebody had hung another dozen along the north rail. The four girls from the wall had a place on the second bench, close together, with their knees drawn up, and the dock partner sat behind them like a cliff behind a beach.

Vell stood at her table on the west side of the floor.

She was not sitting. Her stool was pushed under the table, out of reach, and she stood with the red-taped main-floor book open along her left forearm and the pen held upright in her right hand, like a woman who has been given a candle to keep alight through a long service and means to keep it alight. She did not look at him. He had not expected her to.

Brom was at the north rope, where he had said he would be, with the crowd giving him a little room on both sides the way a river gives room to a rock. He did not wave. He met Cael's eyes once over the rope, and nodded, a small nod, and went back to watching the south end of the floor.

Lira was at the south rope.

He found her across the whole length of the stone, behind the place where the other man would stand. She had left her staff at home for once, and her hands rested on the rope. She was looking at his feet. She looked up when she felt him find her, and her mouth did something very small at one corner, and then she looked at his feet again.

At the back of the room, by the side door, where the light from the lamps came down thinnest, the woman in the grey coat had her book open.

He laid his coat on the north rail and stood on the north mark.

---

Reydan came out of the crowd at the south end without any sign that he was coming.

One moment there was only the crowd, packed to the rope, and the next there was a man stepping over the rope as easily as a farmer steps over a stile. He walked to the south mark. He took off his coat, the plain one that was better than anybody else's, and held it out to his side at arm's length without looking where it went. Somebody in the front row took it, because there was nothing else to be done with a coat held out like that. Then he stood on the mark in his shirt and looked at Cael.

It was the first time Cael had seen him stand up properly. Sitting on the bench, the man had been a shape; standing, he was a weight. He stood square, with his feet a little wider than his hips and his hands loose at his sides, and nothing about the standing told Cael anything at all. There was no favourite foot. There was no little lift of the heel, no settling of the hips, no restlessness in the fingers waiting to become a fist. Cael had seen a thousand fighters stand on a mark, and every one of them had stood ready for something. Reydan stood ready for everything at once. From twelve strides away it made him look like a man who was not ready for anything at all, a man waiting for a cart.

*Good,* thought Cael. *Then I'll not waste the gaze on his feet.*

He put the gaze on him anyway, at full depth, because there was no ration tonight. The room closed in at the edges of his eyes until the lamps on either side of the floor smeared into two pale walls. Ten degrees left and right, and blind past that; he had measured it with chalk in the autumn. He did not need the edges tonight. Everything he needed was on the south mark.

Vell's voice came across the floor without being raised.

"Reydan," she said. "Pressure Path. Iron, on a guild card. Rank eight." The pen moved. "Cael. Iron-equivalent. No Path designation." It moved again. "The rule in this room is that nobody is killed on my floor. Both of you know it. Both of you say so."

"Yes," said Reydan.

"Yes," said Cael.

The room was very quiet now. He could hear the stew pot ticking on its coals in the corner, and somebody's child asking a question and being hushed, and the street's cold coming in at his back.

"Then it's a bout."

Reydan was already moving.

He came straight, without a feint and without any hurry that showed, and covered the first third of the floor in two strides. Cael had planted his left foot before the second stride came down. He did not reach. Brom had said it a hundred times: *plant, arrive, then ask*. He arrived. He asked.

The knock went out from his planted foot, one hard rap on the door of the man in front of him. It came back with a single word: *inward*.

It was the drawing-tight he had felt in Brom every day for eleven days, a whole man becoming a fist in the making. It came from Reydan's right side, low, and it came faster than Brom's ever had. Brom had timed the gap on the post, counting on his own pulse, from the pulling in to the firing: a little less than half a second, four-tenths at the most. Cael had spent a fortnight learning what to do with four-tenths of a second, and he did it now. He shut the knock. He let the hip go.

The Wind took him left and forward in its fan, half a body over, and the burst went through the place where his ribs had been.

He felt it go past. It was not like a fist going past, or a staff. It was like a door slamming very close to his head, a flat hard shove of air that he felt on his cheek and in his ears at once. At the north rope behind him he heard the front row catch its breath and give ground, a whole line of people rocking back on their heels at once. Then the landing beat had him, the two *ands* of the lock, and he stood in it with his weight caught and his breath held and waited for whatever would come into it.

Nothing came. Reydan had not followed. He had stopped, square, and was looking at the place where Cael had landed.

*One.*

The second came off a short step to the left, low, at his knees. *Inward*, said the knock. This time Cael did not trust the hip, because the burst was low and the fan went forward. He went down instead, into the drop that Lira had put into his legs with four hundred repetitions: the left knee, the roll across onto the right foot, and up a body's width to the right with no doorway at the end of it. The burst hit the stone where his shins had been. Chips of the old foundry floor flew and rattled against the rope, and the crowd made a sound like a pot boiling over.

*Two.*

The third he knocked for and got smear, a confused rush of somebody large that did not know which way it was going. He had decided in the alcove what smear meant. He did not go. He stood with his hands up, and Reydan's right arm came forward with nothing in it at all. It was a feint, made with the whole body, and it would have sent a man who moved on a guess straight into the path of the real one. The real one came a heartbeat later, off the same arm. *Inward*, clean, and the hip, and the fan, left, and gone.

*Three.*

The fourth was quick. It came short and early, while he had only half finished landing, as if Reydan wanted to know whether he moved to a count or to something else. He knocked from a foot that had barely found the stone, and the answer came back clean anyway. He went right on his feet, a short ugly step with nothing of Lira's drop in it, and the burst went past his left shoulder close enough to pull at his shirt.

*Four.*

And twice, in among the four, the floor had opened in front of him.

After the third, Reydan's weight sat back on his left heel for the length of a breath, with the ribs on his right side turned toward Cael and nothing in front of them. After the fourth he stood too square and too near for half a beat. Both times Cael saw it with the gaze, as plainly as a gate left open in a wall, and both times he let it close. His right hand stayed where it was. The giving face waited in the hollow under his ribs, three strikes deep, and he kept it asleep. *Not yet.* The room was full of eyes, and every pair of them would go home and write down whatever he showed. He did not mean to show anything until he had to.

He threw nothing back. He stood off and moved, and that was all.

Reydan stepped back. It was a small, formal step, the kind a man takes to say a sentence has ended. He stood a little way from his mark, breathing easily, and looked at Cael with an expression Cael knew from the inside: a man adding something up.

"End of the exchange," said Vell.

---

He went back to the north mark and stood with his hands on his knees and let his breath come back into its count.

The room let its breath go too, all at once, and the sound of it was enormous. Somebody behind the north rope was saying *did you see him go down and come up the other side*, and somebody else was saying *he's not touched him once*. The betting man on his trestle was calling something new in his ruined voice. Cael let all of it go past.

Five knocks, four answered, one smear. Two bursts of the Wind, both left, both clean, and the hip line already warm from the crest to the knee. One drop. One short step right that he would not want to make again. No hits either way.

Four bursts, he thought, and four different ways of asking. Low, high, the feint, the early one. Reydan had not been trying to hit him. He had been finding out how Cael moved, and Cael had told him: left on the hip, right on the feet, and exactly how long the landing lasted, every time, in the lock. The man had stood and watched him land four times. He was not a man who needed telling twice.

*That's the first read,* Cael thought. *He's built it. Now he'll spend it.*

He looked across the stone. Reydan had walked back to the south mark and was standing on it again, square and empty and ready for everything. Over his shoulder, Lira's hands were on the rope.

Cael straightened up.

"Second exchange," said Vell.

Reydan came in from the right this time, angled. Cael planted and knocked, and the knock said *inward*, clean, low on the man's right side, exactly like the first burst of the night. It had the same weight and the same drawing-tight and the same height, and there was nothing in it that he had not felt before. He shut the knock. The hip went. The fan took him left.

He was in the air, half a body over, when he understood.

The burst was not going through the place he had left. It was going through the place where he was about to be. It came low and flat, a hand's breadth inside the spot where he would land, as if the man had aimed at the floor and trusted the floor to have Cael on it by the time the burst arrived. He had been given the old warning and a new aim. And there was nothing to be done about it, because the landing was two *ands* long, and in those two *ands* his legs belonged to the lock and not to him.

He had one thing he could still move, and he moved it. The left forearm came up across his ribs.

The burst struck it halfway between wrist and elbow. He heard it before he felt it: a short dull crack, like a mallet on a wet post. Then the force went on through the arm, and through the arm into him, and took his feet off the stone as if he weighed nothing at all.

He came down on his right side.

The stone was cold. He noticed that first, oddly, before anything else, the old foundry floor pressing its cold up through his shirt into his ribs as it had come up through his boots every night of the autumn. Above him the ceiling was one sheet of small flames from wall to wall, and he lay and looked at it. Somewhere a long way off, six hundred people had made one sound and were still making it.

*Fingers,* he thought, and told them to curl. They curled, slowly, as if the message had to go round by a longer road than usual. From the elbow down the arm was a long ringing numbness with a hot seam through the middle of it. *Not broken. Badly hurt. It guards now. It doesn't block.*

He put his right palm flat on the stone and got up.

---

Lira had known it a breath before he did.

She had watched his feet the whole of the first exchange, the way she had watched them every morning for eleven days in the alcove and every night for a year before that. She watched them the way a woman watches a thing she made, waiting for it to go wrong in the place she knows is weakest. Four times they had been right. Four times she had seen the left hip drop and the body go left and forward in its fan, or the knee go down and the roll come across, and every time her own feet had moved a little inside her boots at the south rope, as if she were the one on the stone.

And four times she had watched the man watch him land.

Nobody else had seen it; she was sure of that. The room had been watching the bursts, because the bursts were the noise and the danger. It had watched Cael vanish and reappear, and gasped, and laughed. But Lira stood almost behind Reydan's shoulder, close enough to see the side of his face, and she had seen where his eyes went after each burst. They did not go to where Cael had been, and they did not go to Cael. They went to the stone a little way in front of the place where he would come down, and they stayed there, very still, while he stood in the lock.

He was counting. She knew it as surely as if he had said the numbers aloud.

She had seen it once before, in the autumn, when the Shield with the tally book had stood in front of Cael and waited for him to land and counted two. Bede had counted it. She had told Cael so herself, afterward, at the landing table in her room with the lamp between them, and the two of them had sat there half the night with his Log open and worked out together what it meant: the lock was a shape and not a habit, a patient man could read it from a bench, and there was nothing Cael could ever do to make it shorter. They had written it in the Log, in his column for the things he could only guard. Then they had built the feet, so that he would never need the hip when he did not know. *The hip only when I know.*

And he had known, each of those four times. He had known when. That was all the knock had ever promised him: the moment, and the moment was enough to go. And going left was the only way the hip ever went.

When the second exchange began and Reydan came in from the right, Lira saw the gathering come into the man before the room did, a tightening down his right side as if somebody had pulled a cord through him. She saw Cael plant. She saw the hip begin to drop.

She saw Reydan's eyes go to the stone a little to the left of where Cael would land. It was the same place, exactly the same, and his eyes stayed there.

Her hands closed on the rope.

She could not call out. Nobody could. The coaching rule was the oldest rule on this floor after the one about killing. Vell had once stood at her table and told a man to his face what it would cost to break it: *you won't fight in any circuit from here to the coast*. If Lira opened her mouth now, the bout would stop. Vell would stop it, and look at her, and write a line about her in the red-taped book that nobody could ever take back. She did not open her mouth.

She stood at the rope and watched the thing she had made carry him exactly where the man had decided he would go.

The burst went in under his landing, and his arm came up, and she heard it hit. It was not a loud sound; it was short and flat, like a sack of grain dropped on boards, and she felt it in her teeth. Then he was in the air without having jumped, and then he was on the stone on his side. The whole room was on its feet and roaring, and Lira found that she had stopped breathing and could not remember how to start.

He lay there.

One breath. Two. She counted them the way she counted for him in the alcove, without meaning to. He lay on his right side with his left arm across his body and his face turned up to the lamps, and he did not move. The room roared. And she thought, very clearly, *if he doesn't get up I'll not be able to keep my hands on this rope*.

His right hand moved. It came down flat on the stone beside his hip, the way a man puts his hand on a table before he stands.

He got up.

He did not get up quickly. He came up one piece at a time, the hand and then the knee and then the other foot, the way she had taught him to come up out of the drop when he was tired, with nothing wasted and nothing hurried. He stood. He held the left arm across his ribs. Then he looked across the floor at Reydan with the face he wore on the bench when he was watching somebody he had not worked out yet. It was as if the stone and the noise and the arm had all happened to someone else and been reported to him by letter.

Lira breathed.

---

It did not get better after that, and she had not thought it would.

Reydan did not give him time to think. He came on, not hard and not even fast, but steadily, and he fired the bursts in pairs now. The first went at Cael's body; the second went, before the first had finished, at the place the first would send him. She watched Cael knock and go, and knock and go. He did not use the hip again. He went on his feet every time, and on his feet he was slower, and every step went on the bill of his legs. She knew those legs better than anybody alive, better than he did. She could see the extra half step where a single step should have done it. She could see the knee that did not quite come all the way up out of the roll. Each one was a coin out of the purse that would have to last him the night.

Once he knocked and stood still, and she knew from his stillness that the knock had come back smeared, and that he was keeping to what he had decided about smear. The burst after it went past his chest by a hand. The one after that took him across the shoulder blade as he turned away, a glancing shove, and sent him three steps toward the rope.

Toward her rope.

She saw what Reydan was doing then, and wished she had not. He was not trying to hit him any more. He was moving him. Every pair went a little to the same side of Cael's body, so that every time Cael went, he went the same way, and the way led to the south rope where she stood. The rope was the end of the floor. A man who went over it had gone out of the bout, and that was forfeit, as plain as a hand raised. Reydan was walking him off the stone the way a drover on the river road walks a frightened beast into a pen, without ever laying a hand on it, only by standing each time in the one place where it does not want to go.

Cael was a stride from the rope. Then less. She could see the back of his neck, wet, and his shoulders going up and down. She could have reached out and touched him.

She kept her hands on the rope.

*Drop,* she thought at him, with all of herself, as hard as she had ever thought anything. *Drop and roll. Right. Off the line. You've got the legs for one. I put it there.*

He did not drop.

He planted, a stride from the rope, and turned his left shoulder to the next burst. She had watched him do it in the alcove against Brom's slow push, three times a session and never more. It was Brom's idea, and Cael had bled for it, and he had told her he would only spend it on a place he meant to stand in. He spent it now, with his left arm hurt and held against his body. The burst went into the turned shoulder and through him at a slant, and it went crooked. She could see the hip catch on it. It went out past him into the rope post beside her, which jumped. And the push of it, going past, shoved him sideways and in, along the rope and off the line, in three long ugly strides with his feet scrambling under him and nothing of her teaching in them at all.

Then he was out of the corner, in the open floor, with his back to nobody, and the crowd made the noise a crowd makes when a thing it was sure of does not happen.

Reydan stopped. He looked at Cael for a moment from where he stood. Then he took his small formal step back.

"End of the exchange," said Vell. Lira heard her pen go down on the page and stay there, longer than it needed to.

Lira looked at her own hands. They were white across the knuckles where she had held the rope, and when she let go they shook, so she pressed them flat against her coat to make them stop. Across the floor at the north rope, Brom was looking at her, not at Cael. He had seen her face, she thought, all the way through. He did not nod. He only looked, steadily, for as long as it took her to look back.

Then she put her hands on the rope again, lightly this time, and went back to watching the feet.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-02-iron-circuit/manuscript/chapter-53.md

# Chapter 53 — The Fourth Exchange

He stood on the north mark with his left arm held against his ribs and made himself count before he let himself think.

Four knocks in the second exchange, three answered, one smeared. One burst of the Wind, the one that had carried him into the man's aim. After that, nothing but feet. One redirect, crooked, bought off the rope with an arm already hurt, and the left shoulder ringing from it along the whole of the groove. That left two in the shoulder by the count he had made in the alcove, and the count had been made for a whole arm. He had half an arm now. *Call it one,* he thought. *Call it one, and be glad if it's two.*

The forearm had settled into a long hot seam from wrist to elbow. The fingers worked if he asked them twice. His legs had paid for every step of the second exchange, and they let him know it now that he was standing still: a shake low in the thighs, a heaviness at the bottom of each breath.

Then Cael let himself think, and found that the thought was very simple.

*The knock doesn't care where.*

He had known it for a week. He had written it in the Log in his own hand: *It gives me when. It doesn't give me where.* But he had known it the way a man knows the river is cold before he falls in. Reydan had shown him the rest of it in one burst. The gathering felt exactly the same whether it was meant for the place he stood or the place he was going, so a gathering he had felt before told him nothing new. Worse, it told him something old, and the old thing was a lie. The man had fired the same pull twice and let Cael's own memory of the first one walk him into the second.

*A repeat is the most dangerous thing he can give me,* he thought. *Because it's the one I'll trust.*

He filed it. It was the only safe place to keep a thing like that in the middle of a floor, in words, put away, where fear could not get at it and spend it for him.

Then he looked at the other column, because the plan had a column, and the plan was the one thing on this floor he had built himself.

*Two.* The first read Reydan had made of him was the map of where he landed. The man had built it in the first exchange and fired it in the second, and it had paid him once: a forearm, where he had priced a set of ribs. He could not fire it again. Cael would never stand in that lock in front of him again while there was a foot left to move on, and Reydan knew it. That read was spent. The second read had been the rope, the long patient walking of a beast into a pen, and Cael had left it by the only door he had, ugly and crooked and paid for in the shoulder. That one was spent too.

Two reads built and thrown away. The plan said three.

And the third, he thought, was the one he had been feeding the man all night, without meaning to: a boy who stood off. A boy who knocked and went and never came back, who had let two open doors close in the first exchange and thrown nothing in eight bursts. Every price Reydan had paid so far had been worked out against that boy. The man had no other.

That was the boy he would have to kill off in the third exchange. Staying alive could wait.

He made himself look at what the job would cost before he took it on, because a plan with no cost column was only a wish. Going in meant the giving face. It meant six hundred people watching a boy who had no Path drive his fists in harder than any boy with no Path could. It meant the woman at the back writing until her pencil wore down, and every man who had come down the river to look at him going home with a page about him in his pocket. Keth had said it in the autumn: *a floor's the most public place in the world*. Bede had shown him what a page could do. Whatever he showed tonight, he would be fighting against it for years.

And if he did not show it, he would lose. He would lose correctly, with everything hidden still hidden, and lie on the stone holding it like a miser holding a purse in a fire.

*Spend it,* he thought. *All of it, if it asks. Just not before it asks.*

He looked once at the north rope. Brom was standing with his arms folded and his face very still, and when their eyes met Brom's chin went down a quarter of an inch and came up again. It was not a nod. It was the thing Brom did in the alcove when Cael said a number aloud and the number was right.

"Third exchange," said Vell.

---

He went first.

He saw it land on Reydan the instant he left the mark: a single blink of the man's whole frame, a weight resettling, the way a man shifts a load he had picked up for one shape and found to be another. For two exchanges the boy across the stone had waited. Now he was coming, and he was not coming straight.

He came on the Wind.

He had never once used it this way on a floor, though Lira had told him a hundred times that it would go if he asked. Evasion, she said, was only footwork, and footwork did not care which way it was facing. He knocked from a planted foot at three strides and got *inward*, and the hip took him left and forward, not away from the burst but across its face and in. The burst went past the place he would have stood if he had been retreating. He landed in the lock two strides out, and Reydan, who had counted that lock four times, fired at it, and the burst went through air, because Cael had landed short on purpose and was already going again on his feet. The second fan took him in the last stride. Then he was inside, a forearm off the man's chest, closer than he had been all night, and close enough to smell the lamp oil on his shirt.

Everything changed at that distance.

The knock had always been best at a forearm. Here it was not a rap on a door but a hand laid flat against one: Reydan's gathering came through to him with a grain to it, a pulling tight that had an edge on one side and not the other, like cloth drawn across a table. He could feel where it was being packed. He could not feel which way it would go, any more than before, but at a forearm's distance there were not many ways left for it to go. And there was one place it could never go at all. A burst went out from the man along a line, and the line had a back to it. Close against Reydan's own firing arm, just behind the elbow where the release began, there was a narrow space the burst would leave as it went, the way the air behind a thrown stone is empty of the stone.

*Inward*, said the knock.

Cael stepped into the empty place.

The burst went off beside his ear. It was so close that he did not hear it as a sound at all, only as a blow on the side of his head that did not touch him, and for a moment the whole left side of the world went silent. And on the beat of it, on the instant the man's frame was given over to sending the force outward and could not be anything else, Cael's right hand was already moving with the giving face behind it.

He hit Reydan in the ribs.

It was like punching a door set solid in its frame. Twelve years of somebody else's money had gone into hardening that side of him, and it showed. But the giving face did not stop at the outside of things. He felt the strike go in, past the hard layer, into the structure underneath. In the same instant the cost came back up his own arm, the knuckles first and then the wrist, bright and sharp, and the hollow under his ribs opened by a third.

*One.*

Reydan gave ground. It was half a pace, no more, and it was not a retreat; Cael knew what it was the moment he saw the back foot slide. A man who fired from the middle of himself needed a little room to fire in, a forearm's worth of air between him and the thing he meant to hit. The half pace was the man making that room. Cael took it away. He went with him, step for step, as close as a partner in a country dance. Reydan slid back again and he followed again, and on the third slide the man stopped trying for room and fired anyway, short and sideways across his own body, a cramped half-burst that had nowhere good to go. It went past Cael's hip. It pulled his shirt half out of his belt.

He hooked the left hand up into the front of Reydan's right shoulder, on the beat of that cramped release.

The left forearm screamed. He had told it it would guard and not block, and now he was asking it to strike, and it told him what it thought of that all the way to the shoulder. But the giving face rode the beat, and the hook landed on the joint where the arm met the body. He felt the man's whole right side jar, and his own knuckles jarred with it, and the hollow opened by another third.

*Two.*

Reydan's breath was right there in front of him now, in his face, warm and smelling faintly of cloves. It still kept its count. But the count had a catch in it on the right side, a small hitch at the top of every breath, exactly over the place the first strike had gone in. For two long beats neither of them could do anything. Cael stayed too close for a burst to be packed. Reydan kept his feet where the Wind could find no angle. They turned on the stone a hand apart, and the room's noise stood over them at its peak like a wave that would not break.

Then the knock said *inward*, low on the right side, and Cael felt the gathering start.

He hit it.

He did not wait for the release, as he had the first two times. He went on the pulling in, the moment the man's whole frame drew toward its middle, and drove his right fist into the meat of Reydan's thigh just above the knee. It was low and plain and entirely within the rule. It was not a blow that ended anything. But it landed in the middle of the gathering, and he felt the gathering break, the way a breath breaks when somebody is struck in the middle of drawing it. The burst never came. Reydan's leg gave an inch, and he caught it, and the inch was there on the stone between them for everybody to see.

*Three.* The hollow was full. He had nothing left on that side and he knew it.

He went out the way he had come in, two steps on his feet and the last on the hip, left, until he was out of reach. There he stood, with his hands throbbing in time with his heart and the left forearm a noise he had stopped attending to.

Reydan let him go.


---

"End of the exchange," said Vell.

For once Cael did not look at the man first. He looked at the room, because he needed one breath in which nothing was trying to hit him, and the room gave it to him.

The betting man had stopped calling. He was sitting down on his own trestle with his chalk in his hand and both columns in front of him, and he was not writing anything in either of them. At the main door Dace had shut his little book and put it away inside his coat. Cael had never once seen him do that in the middle of a bout. On the second bench the four girls from the wall were on their feet, holding on to each other. Behind them the dock partner stood with his broken nose and his big arms folded, and his mouth was moving, and Cael understood after a moment that he was saying the same word over and over: *again, again, again*.

At the south rope, behind Reydan's shoulder, Lira had her hands on the rope and did nothing at all. She could not. He knew what it was costing her, and he looked away from it before it could cost him too.

Reydan had a hand pressed flat against his right side. He took it away as soon as he noticed it was there, the way a man takes his hand out of a pocket he was told as a boy not to keep it in.

*Three,* Cael thought.

He had the plan by heart, and under the plan, in his mind, he had kept a short list all night, three lines long. He went down it now and struck each line through. *Where I land*: struck through in the second, paid for with a forearm. *The rope*: struck through in the second, paid for with a shoulder. *A boy who only stands off*: struck through just now, with three strikes he would feel in his knuckles for a week. Three ways the man had made sense of him, and every one of them made worthless in front of six hundred people.

Then came the plan's promise: the fourth answer would come with no shape to it, and in the shapelessness he would move. A boy had written the plan in a quiet alcove, and he had been honest enough to leave the fourth answer blank.

In the alcove the night before, under the plan, he had written three guesses in the margin and drawn a box round them. *Everything in one.* That was a single burst at full weight, the man's whole store on a line nobody could read. *Hands.* That was the man giving up his Path altogether, closing and gripping and fighting with whatever his body had learned in his father's yard before any academy took hold of him. And *many*: no reading at all, only bursts, cheap and steady, poured onto a boy whose legs had been paying since the first exchange. He had an answer to one of the three. It was the answer he had built for a fortnight in the alcove with Brom's slow push, and it lived in a shoulder that had just been spent, or nearly.

His body was the purse tonight. He went through it coin by coin.

The left forearm was a guard and nothing more. Both hands were bright and stupid from the knuckles to the wrists, and the grip was going. The hollow under his ribs sat full and tight, like a breath he could not let out. The hip line was lit from crest to knee: six asked bursts on the night, three of them in that one exchange, which was the most it would ever give. And there were his legs, the best thing he still had, because Lira had put them there one cold morning at a time. But they were fraying now. The last fan had come down a hand short of the place he had sent it. A burst that fell short by a hand was a door that would not quite shut.

Across the stone, Reydan did something Cael had not seen him do all night.

He brought his feet in. They had stood a little wider than his hips since he first stepped over the rope, ready for everything; now he set them closer, square, almost under his shoulders, the way a man stands to work at a post in a yard. He let his hands hang. He rolled his right shoulder once. And his eyes, which had gone from Cael's feet to his hands to the stone and back all evening, building and checking, came to rest on the middle of Cael's chest and stayed there.

*He's done reading,* thought Cael. *He's going to work.*

"Fourth exchange," said Vell.

---

The first burst came at half weight. The second was gathering before the first had reached him.

Cael went on his feet, because the hip had nothing more to give tonight, and the first went past. So did the second. The third clipped the point of his right hip as he turned and spun him a quarter round. He did not fall, because of a cold morning in the alcove when Lira had stood over him and said, *Get your feet first, and then do anything you like*. He got his feet. The fourth was already gathering.

There was no aim in it, or almost none. That was the cruelty of it. The man had stopped looking for him and was simply pouring bursts onto the place where he was, one after another, none heavy, each costing Reydan a little and each costing Cael a step. Cael had been paying in steps since the first exchange. He thought of the drawings Hesk had sent him in the winter, the mill on Fen Street with its wheel. Every paddle of that wheel came down into the water at exactly the same place on every turn, and none of them was strong enough by itself to move anything, and all of them together moved the whole mill.

That was the thought that saved him, though he did not know it yet.

He was counting. He had counted the landing beat so many times in the alcove, *and one, and two*, that the count went on in him now by itself, under everything, like a clock in another room. And the bursts were landing on it. Each one came down on the *three*. Not near it, but exactly on it, to the grain, every time. A man who had stopped reading had gone back to the oldest thing he owned. And the oldest thing Reydan owned, from a paid yard at ten and an academy at eleven, was a drill, and a drill was regular, and a regular thing could be read without the knock at all.

He planted on the *two*, in the gap, and knocked. *Inward.* On the three. He went. He planted on the next *two* and knocked again. *Inward.* On the three.

Two knocks, both clean, and both exactly where the count had promised.

So now he had the when twice over, from the knock and from the count. And when he looked for it, the where was there as well. Every burst of the fourth exchange had come at his right side. In the first exchange the man had learned that the hip only ever went left. He was not reading any more, but his body still remembered the lesson, and it was setting every burst at Cael's right shoulder and walking him leftward round the floor in a long slow circle until his legs gave out.

He could not outrun a mill. Nobody could; it had been built that way. But a mill did not need outrunning. It needed one paddle caught.

He took the next one on the redirect.

To turn his left shoulder to it he had to turn half his back to the man, and he did, and planted, and let it in. It went through him at the slant the way Brom had taught him in the alcove: shoulder and side and hip, and out through the back heel into the stone. It was clean. It was the cleanest he had ever made, and the push of it going past shoved him a full step inward along a line he had chosen. Then the bill came, all of it, from the top of the left arm to the breastbone. The groove was full. The left shoulder told him there was nothing more in it tonight, in a voice he did not try to argue with.

He was a step closer. It was not enough. On the count, at his right shoulder, the next was gathering.

There was no time to turn the left to it again, and nothing in the left if there had been.

There was the right.

He had never drilled it. Not once in the alcove. Every redirect had gone through the left, because Brom's push only ever came at him straight on and the left was the side he turned. He did not know the line through the right side of his own body. Its price was a blank page. The only true thing he knew was that his legs had one more stand in them, perhaps, and not two. If he spent this burst getting out of the way, there would be nothing left to stand on against the one after it.

*Now,* he thought, *while I can still plant.* And the deciding made the whole room go quiet inside him.

He planted on the *two*. On the *three*, he turned his right shoulder into it.

---

For the first instant it was the drill.

The burst came into the turned shoulder at the angle he gave it and began to run down through him, toward the ribs and the hip and the stone, the way the left had passed a dozen pushes in the alcove. *Let it through,* he thought. *Let it go.* It was the wrong side and a line he had never learned, but it was going, it was going down—

It stopped going down.

Where the change began he could never afterward point to, however often he went back for it. The redirect had been running, and then it was not running; it was gathering. Everything the burst had carried drew together into the joint of his right shoulder and stayed there, packed, as if the shoulder were a socket and the force a thing made to fit it. And then, fitted, it turned in the socket. He felt it turn, the whole weight of Reydan's burst coming round inside his own frame until it faced the other way, back along the line it had arrived by, toward the man who had sent it.

*You've no wall,* Brom had said in the alcove, eleven days ago. *There's nothing in you hard enough to turn a burst back on him.*

It was turned. It was in him, and it was facing home.

And it did not make him do anything. That was the part he would remember most clearly, later, when everything else about the fourth exchange had blurred. The thing in his shoulder did not push. It waited, the way a held door waits for somebody to decide whether to go through. He had perhaps the length of a heartbeat, and in it he could see both roads as plainly as the two forks of a lane. Every rule he had ever written for himself said *let go*. He had never once used a thing in a bout that he had not first tried and priced and written down, and this had no page in any book he owned. He did not know what it was, or where it had come from, or what it would cost. Letting go was easy. He had only to stop holding, and the weight would spill out of him somewhere harmless, into the floor or the air. Then he would be standing on empty legs in front of a mill that was already gathering its next paddle, and he would lose, slowly and properly, with every rule he had ever made still whole.

He drove forward.

He did not reach for the thing in his shoulder. He did not aim it or ask it for anything. He only went in, right shoulder first, along the line, into the space the burst had crossed on its way to him, with his knees shouting at every inch. When the shoulder met Reydan's chest it was not a strike. He had no word for it in any column. The burst went home. It went back into the chest that had made it, along the line it had come by, with Cael's weight in behind it, and he felt the meeting run through the two of them like a blow on one end of a long beam felt at the other.

The price came in the same instant. The right shoulder paid for all of it: everything the drill had promised the stone would carry, the joint carried instead. The arm went dead to the fingertips. And the shock of two men's weight meeting in one place came back down through him into his legs and tried, very hard, to fold them.

He locked his knees.

He did it on purpose. Of all the choices in the bout it was the last, and he made it with the bill in front of him. There would be no give and no roll, no soft way down to spare the joints. He set both knees straight and held them there like gate bars dropped into their brackets, because if he went down now he would go down beside the man. Two fighters on the stone together was not a win in Vell's book or in anybody's. It was a line nobody would ever remember right.

His sight went white round the rim. His knees held.

Reydan went down.

He did not go like a man who has been thrown. He went the way a stack of grain sacks goes when the bottom one is pulled out, all at once and straight down and then over, onto his right side on the stone. His own burst had come back to him through the place on his right side where the first strike had already been. He was not out. Cael could see that he was not out, because at once, before the noise of his landing had finished, he began to get up. An elbow came under him. A knee drew up and slid back. The elbow went again, slower, and the body under it would not answer. His mind was perfectly clear; you could see it giving the orders. Then, quite deliberately, as if he had decided it himself, he laid his head down on the cold stone and stayed there, breathing.

The Ironyard was silent.

Six hundred people, and Cael could hear the stew pot ticking on its coals in the corner. He could hear a lamp on the beam above him spit once. Cael's knees were still locked. His right arm hung at his side like a rope off a post. Whatever had turned in his shoulder was gone, completely, as if it had never been there at all, and had left nothing behind it that he could take hold of and look at. He did not move. He was not sure what moving would show him.

Vell's voice came across the stone exactly as it had come across it every night for thirty years, the voice she used to read out a date.

"Iron-equivalent, Cael. Win. Method: forced incapacitation, fourth exchange."

Her pen went down. The room came apart.

---

It came apart from the edges inward. The beams went first, men up there stamping on the old timbers until dust came down through the lamplight like flour. Then the benches, and then the rope, and then the street outside the open doors, which could not see and had to be told and roared when it was told. One of the eel-market men was beating his ladle on the side of his pot, over and over, with a face of perfect happiness. The four girls from the wall were crying, or laughing, or both. The betting man stood up on his trestle and wiped both columns off his board with his sleeve, slowly, from top to bottom, and then stood there with nothing written anywhere. On the east bench the river-academy coat had taken his hands off his knees for the first time Cael had ever seen. He was holding them out in front of him, palms up, as if to show somebody they were empty.

Cael did not hear his name in it. He heard it, but it did not land. What landed was smaller.

He tried to count, and found he could not start where he always started.

He always began at the top: the hip, then the hands, then down. Tonight his attention went to the right shoulder and stayed there, and he could not make it go anywhere else, because he did not know what he was looking at. It did not hurt like a bruise, or like the groove in the left, or like anything with a name. It was hollow. It felt like the flue of a chimney the morning after a chimney fire, when the stone is still warm all the way up and blackened by something that went through it faster and hotter than it was built to carry.

So he made himself go back to the top and count the things he knew.

The hip line, lit, six asked bursts. The left forearm, a long dark seam from the wrist to the elbow, which tomorrow would be every colour Lira had a word for. Both hands, which would not close all the way. The hollow under the ribs, full and aching. The left shoulder, two redirects deep, the groove alight. His legs, shaking now that he had let them. His knees, which he had locked, and which would send him their bill in the morning, itemised.

Then, again, the right shoulder.

There was no entry for it. He had not called it. He had not aimed it or wanted it; he had not known there was anything there to want. He had only gone where it was already pointing. That was the whole of what he had done, and he held on to that, because it was true and he could stand on it. Everything else about the last half second was a door he did not mean to open in the middle of the Ironyard with six hundred people watching.

*Whatever that was,* he thought, *it isn't in the book.* And he left it there.

He unlocked one knee, slowly, and then the other. They held.

On the stone in front of him, Reydan had begun to move again. He did it in order, one part and then the next, the way a careful man unpacks a box he means to put everything back into.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-02-iron-circuit/manuscript/chapter-54.md

# Chapter 54 — On the Stone

Reydan lay on the stone and took stock of himself, because that was what you did first, before anything else, before you even thought about the man who had put you there.

He had been taught it at eleven by a master with a bad hip who had lost more bouts than any man in the academy and taught more winners than all the rest together. *When you go down,* the old man had said, *you are a clerk before you are a fighter. Count what you have. Then decide what to do with it.* Reydan had gone down perhaps twenty times in eleven years of floors, and every one of those times he had lain still and counted. He counted now.

The ribs on the right side, low, under the arm. That was the first strike, the one that had gone in through the hard of him as if the hard were not there; and then the last one, whatever the last one had been, through the same place. Something in there clicked when he breathed past halfway. Not broken, he thought. Cracked, perhaps. A month of sleeping on his left side and not laughing.

The front of the right shoulder, where the joint was. That was the hook, the left hand, which had come up from below on the beat of his own release when he had been certain the boy's left arm was finished. It had not been finished. It had only been put away, which was a different thing, and he made a note of that, a note to himself in the place he kept such notes.

The right thigh above the knee. That had been the low one, into the gathering. It had not hurt much at the time, and now the whole muscle had gone dull and heavy and would not do what he told it.

And under all of it, a deep plain shaking that he knew very well and did not like: his body reporting that it had been sent to fetch something heavier than itself, and had fetched it anyway.

His mind was perfectly clear. That was the strange thing. He had given his body three orders since he landed, *elbow, knee, up*, and it had heard all three and done none of them. So he had stopped giving them and laid his head down on the stone, which was cold, colder than he would have thought a floor could be in a room this hot, and he lay there and listened.

There was no sound at all. Then there was a woman's voice saying his name and the boy's name and a rating and a method. Then there was a noise like a roof falling in.

*Well,* thought Reydan. *That's that.*

He found that it did not feel like any loss he remembered. He had lost once in the river halls, years ago, to a man who would not come out and play, and that had felt like being cheated, though he had known even at the time that it was nothing of the sort. This did not feel like that. This felt more like reaching the bottom of a staircase in the dark and finding one more stair than you had counted. He was not angry. He was surprised, and under the surprise there was something he had not felt on a floor for a long time, and it took him a moment to know it for what it was. It was interest.

He began to get up.

He did it the way he had been taught to get up after a bad fall, one part at a time, asking each part before he trusted it. The left elbow first, which answered. The left knee, which answered. The right leg, which grumbled and then answered. He came up to sitting and stayed there with one hand flat against his right side, taking a few breaths that stopped short of the click, and then he got his feet under him and stood. He stood for a while longer than he needed to, letting the room settle and his legs decide that they were legs.

The boy was standing in front of him.

He was a pace and a half away, no more, and he had not moved since the end. His right arm hung at his side, and the way it hung told Reydan that it was no use to him at all just now. His left was held across his ribs. He was very pale under the lamps, and he was breathing in short careful pulls, the way a man breathes when every breath has a price. And he was looking at Reydan with exactly the look he had worn on the east bench the night before: patient, steady, unhurried, reading.

Reydan had spent eleven years on floors, and he knew most of the things that could be done on one. He had been stopped by an Iron Skin who hardened at the exact instant his burst arrived; he had been turned aside by a Pressure man who saw it early enough to step off the line and take it on the slant. He had never once had his own burst come back to him. Not turned aside, not stopped, not spread into the floor. Sent back, along its own line, into the place it had come out of, with all its weight still in it. He knew the weight of his own burst better than he knew the weight of anything in the world. He had been hit by his own work.

"What Path is that?" he said.

"I don't have one."

"That's not what I asked."

"I know."

The boy did not look away when he said it, and he did not look pleased with himself either. He said it the way a man tells you the road is out ahead, plainly, because it is true and you will need to know it.

Reydan found that he believed him. He did not know what he believed; only that the boy was not lying, and that whatever the true answer was, the boy did not have it either. He looked at him a moment longer, running the fourth exchange through again behind his eyes, and it still came out at the same place: a turned shoulder, and then nothing he had a name for.

He let it go, for now. There was something else he wanted to say, and he wanted to say it before the room came back.

"There's a woman at the back by the side door," he said. "Grey coat. A book." He did not turn his head to look; he did not need to. "She's been writing about you since before I sat down last night. I know the kind of book. I've been in a few. People who write in books like that do it for somebody, and they don't stand at the back of a hall like this one for the pleasure of it." He paused. "I thought you'd want to know. I think you know already."

"I know."

"Good." Reydan breathed, short of the click. "Find me later. I want the answer when you have it."

He turned away. A man in the front row of the crowd was holding out a coat to him, a good plain coat, with both hands, as if it were something that might break. It took Reydan a moment to remember that it was his own.

---

He walked home by the river road because it was the longer way, and because he did not want to be in a street full of people telling each other what they had seen.

The night was very cold and very clear. The lamps of the district went down the hill behind him and the river went along beside him, black and quick and talking to itself, and he walked slowly with his coat buttoned to the throat and one hand inside it, against his ribs. Two or three people passed him going the other way and looked at him, and one of them looked twice and opened his mouth, and Reydan looked back at him pleasantly until he shut it again.

He had been beaten once before, in the river halls in his second season, by a man who would not come out and play. There had been perhaps two hundred in that hall. He remembered the faces turning toward the other man as the fifth exchange began, the way a field of barley turns when the wind changes, and the cold feeling of being, all at once, not the thing everyone had come to watch. He had felt that cold again tonight, on the stone, and it was not as bad as he remembered. It was, if anything, rather clean.

The saddler's was dark. He let himself in at the side door with the key the saddler's wife had given him, and climbed the narrow stairs one at a time, and lit the lamp in his room and sat down on the edge of the bed to take off his boots. It took a long time. The right leg did not want to bend, and the right side did not want him to lean.

When he had them off, he did not lie down. He sat with his hands on his knees and ran the bout back from the beginning, the way his masters had taught him to do with every bout he had ever fought, won or lost, before he slept.

The first exchange. He had come straight, to see how the boy moved, and the boy had moved four ways in four bursts and thrown nothing. There had been two openings, and the boy had seen both and let both go. Reydan had seen him see them. At the time he had taken it for caution. Now, sitting on the bed, he did not think it had been caution at all. He thought it had been a boy buying something.

The second. He had fired into the landing, because the landing was the one thing about the boy that never changed: two counts, every time, a shape you could hang a burst on like a coat on a peg. It had been the best idea of his night. It had cost the boy an arm and put him on the floor. And then the boy had got up, in pieces, carefully, and looked at him across the stone with no expression at all, as if he had been sent a bill he had been expecting.

He had walked him to the rope, and the boy had gone out of it sideways, crooked and ugly and through a burst that ought to have stopped him. *He paid for that,* Reydan thought. *He paid for every step of that. He chose to pay.*

The third. The boy had come.

Reydan sat for a long time with that. The whole of his evening had been built on a boy who stood off. Every burst he had fired had been fired at that boy, and every price he had paid had been reckoned against him. And then the boy had come across the floor on the same strange sideways step he had used to get away, so that Reydan had fired at a place a retreating boy would have been and found nobody in it. Then he had been inside, closer than anyone stood to Reydan by choice, close enough to step into the one place a burst cannot go. And he had hit him three times, with a weight that did not belong in a boy that size, on the beat every time.

*He made me build three pictures of him,* Reydan thought, *and he made me spend all three.* He had heard of fighters who did that. He had never met one. He had always been the one doing it.

And so he had stopped making pictures. He had gone to the post. Twelve years of his life had been spent at one post or another, in his father's yard and the academy's, pouring out bursts on a count until he could do it asleep, and when the pictures ran out he had gone back there without even deciding to. And the boy had heard the count. Reydan was sure of it. He had seen the boy's lips move, once, in the middle of the fourth exchange, as if he were counting along.

And then the turned shoulder. And then the thing he had no name for.

He went back to it three times, sitting on the edge of the bed with his boots on the floor beside him, and three times he came to the same place and could go no further. A Pressure man could turn a burst aside. An Iron Skin could stop one dead. Nobody he had ever heard of could do what had been done to him, and the boy who had done it had stood there afterward and told him, honestly, that he had no Path at all.

There were two ways to carry a thing like that home.

He had seen the first. One autumn the other house had withdrawn its boy before the final of the invitational, over a wrist that was perfectly well, and the boy had never forgiven Reydan for a bout that never happened. He had let it go bad in him. He had come back the next year with a grievance instead of a plan, and then not come back at all. Reydan had seen men in the river halls do the same with a single bout, keep it like a stone in a shoe for years. They walked a little crooked all their lives and blamed the road.

The second way was harder, and he had only ever seen it done once or twice, by masters. It was to be more interested than you were hurt.

He sat in the lamplight in the room above the saddler's and found, a little to his own surprise, that he had already chosen. He had chosen it on the stone, with his cheek on the cold floor, at the moment he had thought *that's that* and found that what he felt was interest. He had come three days down the river on a wool wagon because a keeper in his own city had heard a story about a boy with no Path. He had wanted to know whether the story was true. It was truer than the story. And he still did not know what it was.

*Then I'll ask,* he thought. *Not tonight. Not this year, perhaps. But I'll ask, and I'll keep asking until he can tell me.*

He lay down carefully on his left side, and put out the lamp, and lay a long time in the dark listening to the river and to the small click in his ribs, before he slept.

---

Lira got to him first.

She came under the south rope without asking anybody's leave and across the stone at a walk that was very nearly a run, and she did not say anything at all. She put her hands on him. She went down his left forearm from the wrist to the elbow, pressing, watching his face. She went to the ribs on both sides, and then to the left shoulder and the right. At the right shoulder she stopped, because he could not quite keep his face still. She looked at him, and took her hand away, and put it back again more gently, flat, the way you put your hand on a horse that has been frightened.

"You did it," she said.

"Yes."

She did not say anything else for a moment. She stood with her hand on his shoulder and looked at him, and he saw that her face had been somewhere he had not been, the whole of the second exchange and the whole of the fourth, at a rope she could not cross with a mouth she could not open. He saw it come back, slowly, from wherever it had been.

"Your arm," she said.

"It's not broken."

"I know it's not broken. I felt it." She took her hand away. "It's going to be the colour of a plum by midnight, and you're not going to be able to hold a cup with that hand tomorrow, and I'm going to have to hold it for you, and I'm going to enjoy it."

Brom had come round by the north rope. He stood a little way off, not crowding, with his arms folded, and looked at Cael the way he had looked at him from three benches back on the first night they ever sat in the same room, as if Cael were a page that had turned out to have writing on the back of it.

"What did it feel like?" said Brom.

Cael thought about it. He thought about it honestly, because Brom had asked honestly, and because it was the first question anybody had asked him since the end that he could answer. He went back through the fourth exchange: the mill, and the count, and the left shoulder full, and the right shoulder turning; the moment at the fork, and his knees, and the half second afterward when he had not known whether they would hold.

"Like I had enough."

It was true. He knew it the moment it was out of his mouth. It was not that he had won. It was that for once, at the very bottom of everything he had, there had been enough.

Brom nodded slowly, as if Cael had given him a figure and the figure was right.

"Sit down," said Lira. "Before you fall down. Sit down here, on the stone, and don't argue with me."

He sat down on the stone.

---

The floor did not clear quickly. It took the better part of an hour, and for most of that hour Cael sat at the edge of the north mark with his back against the rail post and his left arm across his knees, and let it go past him.

People came up to the rope and looked at him and went away again. Some of them said things. The dock partner came and put one enormous hand on the top of the rail post above Cael's head, and left it there a moment, and went away without a word. Ansel came, with his coat buttoned to the throat, and stood at the rope for some time looking at the middle of the floor where Reydan had lain. Then he looked at Cael and nodded, once, a whole sentence in it, and went out by the side door into the cold. Vell did not come. She stood at her table with the red-taped book open and wrote, and wrote, and turned a page, and wrote on. Dace went round the walls with the doorkeeper, gently moving people toward the doors like a man herding hens.

Lira sat beside him on the stone with her shoulder almost against his and did not talk. Brom stood. Brom watched the doors.


"You looked at me," said Brom, after a while, to the doors. "In the third. Before you went."

"I did."

"I nodded."

"You didn't nod. You did the thing with your chin you do when I say a number aloud and it's right."

Brom thought about that. "I didn't know I did that."

"You do it every day."

"Then I'll go on doing it," said Brom, "now that I know what it's for." His eyes came round to Cael at last. "Put it in the book, with the rest. A man ought to know what his own chin's saying."

Cael was going to answer him, and then he saw the woman in the grey coat.

She was standing three strides off, at the edge of the stone, with her small book shut in one hand, and he had not seen her come. He had been watching the doors with Brom, and the sweepers, and the stew pots going out, and somehow in all that watching she had crossed half the floor without once being a thing he looked at. He knew how much practice that took. He had been trying to learn it himself for a year. She was waiting, without any sign of impatience, for him to finish seeing her before she said a word.

He finished. She was perhaps forty. Her coat was a good coat but a travelling one, soft at the cuffs and the hem from long use, and her boots had walked a great deal of road and very little floor. Her hands had no marks on them. She stood like a woman who had stood at the backs of a great many rooms and been noticed in none of them, because she had arranged it so.

Lira's shoulder went still beside his. Brom unfolded his arms.

"My name is Quenna," said the woman. "I'm a senior teaching-practitioner at Greyvane Academy. Silver-tier." She said it without any weight, as if she were telling him the time. "And what I do, mostly, is this: I specialize in irregular Path development assessment."

---

He made himself sit up straighter against the post, though it cost him, because he wanted to see her face while she talked.

"You've been here before," he said.

"Six nights, counting tonight." She did not look at her book. "On the first two I watched every bout on the card. Something had come up the river to us, about this hill, and I came to see whether it was a fighter or a story. By the end of the second night I had stopped watching the card. The other four nights were for you." She tipped her head a little toward the side door. "Your Circuit Master knew my pass came from an academy. Tonight, at the door, I showed him which one. I thought he'd earned that."

"Four nights," said Cael.

"Watching what you do with a thing the first time you do it." She seemed to find nothing odd in saying so. "That's my work, all of it. I keep a drawer at Greyvane with eleven years of notes in it, about fighters on floors like this. Most of them showed me everything they had on the first night, and every night after that they showed me the same thing a little better. You've shown me something new every time I've come. Tonight you showed me more than I've written down in a year." She turned the closed book over once in her hand. "I'm not here because I was curious. Curiosity I can feed at home. I came down to make you an offer."

She laid it out the way a woman lays a table, one thing at a time, each in its place.

Greyvane's charter held a provision, very old, which nobody there had ever had much use for. It had been written for children whose ability showed itself before any Arbiter had looked at them, so that the academy could take a child on what she could do and not wait on a paper that did not exist yet. It was called the demonstration-provision track. For as long as anybody remembered, it had been used for those children and for nobody else.

"But it doesn't say children," said Quenna. "It doesn't say *unkindled*. I've been through it word by word more often than I'd like to admit, and the words are these: *any practitioner without a standard Arbiter-issued classification*." She let the words stand by themselves on the stone between them. "That is you. I don't need to know why it's you. There are people at Greyvane who would like to know whether that sentence means what it says, and I am the one who has been saying for years that it does. I'd like you to be the proof."

There was something under the last words that was not quite pride. Cael heard it and put it away for later. She was not reading him a rule. She had an argument, she had been carrying it for years, and she meant to win it.

"The Compact keeps a file on me," he said, at once, plainly. He did not want her to find it out later and wonder why he had hidden it.

"We know that." Her voice did not change. "There are people in the building whose whole work is that sort of trouble. They have handled it before, and they will handle it again after you."

"My classification—"

"No." She lifted a hand, very slightly, and he stopped. "Nobody at Greyvane will ask you what you are. That isn't what the provision is for. Once a month you will stand in front of two of the assessment staff and show them what you can do, and they will write down whether it reaches the standard. That is all. Month after month, in front of people who know what they are looking at." Her eyes went once to the middle of the floor, where the chalk of the south mark was scuffed nearly away. "Tonight would reach it with a good deal to spare."

"And the month it doesn't?"

"Then we sit down, you and I, and find out what's changed, before anyone decides anything." Something sharpened in her voice for the first time, very slightly, the way a knife catches the light when it is turned. "The ordinary road through an academy is a gate with one question at it, asked once, and if you can't answer the question you never get as far as showing anybody anything. Most of the names in my drawer are carters now. A few are dead. I think that's a waste, and I've said so to the masters for eleven years, often enough that some of them have stopped inviting me to supper."

"How many?" said Lira. "That you've brought in."

Quenna looked at her properly for the first time. It was long and unhurried, such as a woman gives when she has already read a page about somebody and wants to see how close the page came.

"Three, in eleven years. One stayed a season and went home to keep bees. There was nothing wrong with his fighting; he simply couldn't bear a bell telling him when to eat, and I ought to have seen that in him before I asked. Two stayed. One of those two teaches now." She looked back at Cael. "Two masters and an afternoon, every month, for every one of you. That's what you cost us. I've never yet thought it too much."

Cael turned it over, looking for the catch, the way he would have looked for the real fist behind a feint. He could not find one. That told him more than anything she had said. A woman selling a dream would have rounded the numbers. She would have left out the man with the bees. She would not have said *every month* as if it were a weight she wanted him to feel before he picked it up.

"What about expelled practitioners?" said Lira.

She said it flat and careful, in the voice of somebody who has taught herself not to let a thing matter until she has heard the answer. Quenna did not need to open her book.

"Wind Path, Copper-tier, expelled from Fenmark Academy."

"Yes."

"There's a second provision. Re-certification. It's a different door, and it opens into the same rooms. It asks you to be assessed every month as well, and every month to show that you've gone further than the month before." Quenna paused. "It is not a kind road."

"I can do that."

"I thought you could," said Quenna.

There was something nearly warm in it, under the plainness, and it was gone almost before it arrived. Lira did not move. But Cael, beside her, felt something in her shoulder let go by a hair, like a rope eased a single turn on its cleat.

Brom shifted his weight from one foot to the other. Quenna's eyes went to him and rested there, for about as long as it takes to read a name off a list. Then they moved on. She said nothing to him at all. Brom's face did not change.

"I'm at the inn at the top of the market row," said Quenna. "Take three days. Come and tell me what you've decided, whichever way it goes." She looked at each of the three of them in turn and inclined her head to each. Then she turned and went back across the stone. Within a dozen steps she was one more coat going out at the side door, and then she was not even that.

Nobody said anything for a while. At the far end of the floor the sweepers had begun. Behind them, the eel-market men were scraping the last of the stew out of their pots.

"She wasn't surprised by a single one of your questions," said Brom.

"No," said Cael.

"She's done that before."

"Or she spent four nights getting ready for us."

"Both," said Brom, and went back to watching the doors.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-02-iron-circuit/manuscript/chapter-55.md

# Chapter 55 — The Accounting

They went up the hill slowly, because there was no other way for him to go up it.

Lira had made a sling for the left arm out of her scarf before they left the building, tying it at the back of his neck with a knot she said she had learned on a barge and would not explain. The right arm hung. He had tried, at the foot of the steps, to lift it far enough to put his hand on the rail, and it had come up as far as his hip and stopped there, politely, like a dog that has been told to go further than it wants to. So he climbed with no hands at all, Lira on one side of him and Brom on the other, near enough to catch him and not so near that anybody watching would think they had to.

Plenty of people were watching. The street outside the Ironyard was still full, and it did not empty as they passed through it; it opened, and then closed again behind them, and went on talking. Somebody shouted his name from an upstairs window and then, embarrassed, shut the window. A man selling hot chestnuts at the corner held one out to him as he went by and, when Cael could not take it, gave it to Lira instead, and then gave her a second one for herself.

All the way up, the street talked about him as he went through it. It did not talk to him; nobody stopped him or touched his arm. But he heard the bout going up the hill ahead of them and behind them in pieces, a man telling the third exchange to a woman in a doorway with his hands, two boys arguing on a step about whether it had been the shoulder or the knees, somebody at an upstairs window saying *six hundred* to somebody inside as if the number were the whole story. He found that he did not mind it, and was too tired to wonder why.

"I'm going to get fat off you," said Lira.

"You can have mine as well."

"I've already had yours."
