At the top of the market steps Brom stopped. He had his lodging at the carters' inn, which was the other way.

"I'll come in the morning," he said. "Early. Not to do anything. To look at the shoulder." He stood a moment, as if there were something else he meant to say, and then did not say it. Cael could see him decide not to, the way he sometimes decided not to send a push in the alcove: a small settling of the big frame. "Sleep on the left side," said Brom, and went off down the hill.

The boarding house was dark except for the lamp in the front window, which the heavyset man always left burning when anybody in his house was out at the Ironyard. He was sitting up beside it in his shirtsleeves, and he heard them on the step and opened the door before Lira had her hand on it.

"Win?" he said.

He had asked it every bout night for a year and more, standing in that doorway. Cael had said *win* and *lost* and once *I don't know yet*, and the heavyset man had nodded at each answer exactly the same amount.

"Win," said Cael.

The heavyset man looked at the scarf, and the arm hanging out of the sleeve on the other side, and at Cael's face. He nodded exactly the same amount as always. Then he stood back to let them pass and said, to nobody in particular, "There's a bowl on the stair," and went back to his chair by the lamp.

There was a bowl on the stair, covered with a cloth, as there had been every night for a fortnight. Under the cloth there was a stew that had been hot an hour ago, and under the bowl there was a plate, and on the plate, in charcoal, in the sister's large uneven hand, *EAT. L. SAYS.*

"I didn't say anything," said Lira. "I haven't said anything to her in two days." She picked it up. "Come on. I'll hold the spoon."

---

She held the spoon, and he ate, sitting on the edge of his bed with the bowl on her knee, because there was nothing else to be done about it. It was humiliating for about four mouthfuls and then it was not, and by the end of the bowl they were both laughing, though neither of them could have said at what. Then she put the bowl on the crate desk and looked at him.

"You'll write it now," she said. "Before you sleep."

"I can't hold the pen."

"Can you hold it at all?"

He tried the left hand. The fingers came out of the end of the sling and closed round the pen, slowly, as if the message had to travel a long way to reach them, and held it. They could not have held a cup. They could hold a pen, just.

"Then you'll write it," said Lira. "Badly. And in the morning you'll be glad you did." She went to the door between their rooms and stopped there, with her hand on the frame. "I'm through the wall. Knock if you need me. Not the other knock," she added. "The ordinary kind."

When she had gone he sat with the Power Log open on the crate desk under the lamp, and the pen in the fingers that came out of the sling, and he did what he had always done after a floor. He began with the numbers, because the numbers could not be argued with.

The hand was so bad that he could hardly read it himself. He wrote slowly, three or four words and then a rest, like a man crossing a stream on stones.

*Reydan. Main floor. Won, fourth exchange. Forced incapacitation. Vell's words.*

*Knocks: fourteen. Answered twelve. Smeared two (first, second). Of the twelve: nine on bursts I got out of the way of, or tried to; two on the count in the fourth; one on a gathering I hit before it fired. One answered clean and still put me on the floor: the second exchange's first. It gave me when. It was the right when. I went where I always go.*

*Wind: six, all asked, all left. Two in the first, one in the second (the one he fired into), three in the third (in, in, out). None in the fourth. Nothing left.*

*Feet: one drop, right, first exchange. The rest of the second on feet alone. They paid.*

*Giving face: three, third exchange, all on his beat. Ribs, front of the right shoulder, thigh above the knee, the last on a gathering, which broke it. Hollow full. Both hands paid.*

*Redirects: three. First, off the rope, left shoulder, crooked: the forearm was already hit. Second, fourth exchange, left, clean, the cleanest I've made; it bought a step and the left was done. Third, fourth exchange, right shoulder. Never drilled on the right. Not once.*

There the pen stopped, lifted, while the lamp hissed.

Then, because the Log did not get to keep only the comfortable things, he wrote what the third had been.

*The third did not stay a redirect. It started as one, going down through me toward the floor, and then it stopped going. It gathered in the right shoulder and turned and went back out along the line it came in by, into him, and I went in behind it. I didn't call it. I didn't aim it. I didn't know it was there to call. I had a moment when I could have let it go, and I went in instead. That's all I did. The going in was mine. The rest wasn't.*

*Cost: the right shoulder, all of it. Arm dead to the fingers for the length of the bout and after. Now: hollow in the joint, like a flue after a fire. Not a bruise. Not the groove. Not anything I have a word for.*

*Knees: locked, on purpose, so I'd be the one standing. They'll send their bill tomorrow.*

He read it back. Then he turned the page and wrote the other part, the part that went in the plan's column, because the plan had been his and he owed it an honest reckoning.

*The plan held. Three reads, and he dropped every one: where I land; the rope; a boy who only stands off. The fourth came with no read in it, and it was the one of my three guesses I had an answer for. Volume, on a count. The count was the thing I could read. Hesk's mill wheel, every paddle in the same place every turn.*

*But the answer I'd built wasn't enough. I'd built three redirects for a shoulder that had already been hit. If the third had only gone through me at the slant, the way the drill goes, I'd have been a step closer with nothing left in either arm and nothing left in my legs, and the next paddle was already coming. I'd have lost. Slowly, properly, with the plan right and me wrong.*

*Something else finished it. I don't know what.*

The lamp hissed. The sentence sat on the page and would not get any longer.

---

Then he turned to the back of the book, past the two columns and *Carrying*, to the page headed *Anomalies*.

The session-nine entry was there, in his ordinary hand, written four days ago at this same desk. He read it through slowly, all of it, the three possibilities and *Evidence insufficient* and *Filed as anomaly. Monitor.* And under it, in the small field at the foot that he had kept for the things that would not go anywhere else, the line he had written the night before the bout: *Session nine. Could not reproduce. Still don't know what that was.*

He wanted very badly for the two to be the same thing.

He noticed himself wanting it, and that was how he knew he had to be careful. It would have been so tidy. Two strange things in four days, both in the same alcove's work, both arriving from nowhere, both with no notice and no name; put them together and there would be one strange thing instead of two, and one was easier to carry. He made himself lay them side by side on the page, in his head, the way he would have laid two fighters' bouts side by side on a bench, and look at where they were not alike.

In session nine there had been no space at all between knowing Brom was gathering and meeting it; the knowing and the meeting had been one movement. This had not. Tonight there had been the knock, and the count, and the plant, and the turned shoulder, every step of it his own and in its order; whatever had happened had happened after all of that, inside a thing he already had.

Session nine had met the gathering itself, before anything was sent, and the push had come apart in Brom's hands. Tonight the burst had been sent, and had arrived, and had been inside him, whole, with all its weight, and had been sent back.

Session nine had cost nothing that he could find, and that had frightened him more than anything all fortnight. Tonight had cost him the whole of a shoulder.

They were not alike. They were not alike in any way he could put his finger on except that he did not understand either of them.

He wrote it in, under its own line, as carefully as the hand would let him.

*The half-second, fourth exchange, Reydan. A redirect on the right shoulder (never drilled) that stopped passing the burst and gathered it, turned it, and sent it back along its own line. Not called; went in on it. Cost: the whole right shoulder, and still costing.*

*Not session nine. Laid them side by side. Session nine: no gap between read and answer, met the gathering before the push, the push came apart, cost nothing. Tonight: every step mine and in order, the burst arrived whole, went back whole, cost everything the shoulder had. Different in every place I can look. The only thing they share is that I don't understand them, and that's not a thing that makes two things one.*

*Filed separately. Monitor both.*

Session nine's entry he left alone, and he kept the nib well away from it, as if ink could spread. He left its *Note* line exactly as he had written it, the night before, in a hand that had been steadier than this.

Then he capped the ink and sat for a while with the book open under the lamp and his left fingers aching round nothing. The fear did not come, which surprised him. He had expected it to, the way it had come after session nine: the cold dread of a thing given away for nothing. But tonight's had not been given away. Tonight's had been paid for, all of it, in the shoulder he could not lift. It had a price, though he could not read the figure yet, and a thing with a price on it was a thing he had lived beside all his life.

Through the wall he heard Lira turn over in her bed, and then lie still.

He blew out the lamp.

---

Brom came at first light, as he had said he would, and knocked at the street door, and was let in by the heavyset man, who had evidently not been to bed at all.

Cael was awake. He had been awake since the knees woke him, an hour before the light, with a long, deep, itemised ache from the hip to the ankle on both sides, exactly as he had known they would. He was sitting on the edge of the bed in his shirt, looking at the left forearm. Lira had been right. It was the colour of a plum from the wrist to the elbow, darker in the middle, where the burst had hit the bone, and the middle was hot when he touched it. The fingers would close to a loose fist and not further.

Brom stood in the doorway and looked at him, and then at the arm, and then at the right shoulder.

"May I?" he said.

"You may."

Brom came and put one big hand flat on the right shoulder, over the joint, very lightly, and kept it there. Cael could feel him reading. It was not like the knock; it was slower and broader, a whole palm's listening, the way Brom read everything, from the front.

His hand stayed there through twenty of Cael's breaths.

"It's quiet," he said at last. "That's all I can tell you. The left's loud: it's all bruise and groove, I can feel it from here, it's like standing next to a smithy. The right's quiet." He took his hand away and looked at it, as he had looked at his hands in the alcove after session nine. "Not dead. I'd know dead. Quiet the way a room's quiet after a lot of people have just gone out of it."

"Is that bad?"

"I don't know. I've never felt a shoulder like it." He sat down on the end of the bed, which complained. "I'll tell you what I felt on the floor, if you want it. Only what I felt, mind. I was at the north rope; I was too far off to read anything much. But at the end, when you went in, I felt something big go past me in the air, going the wrong way." He was quiet a moment. "I've stood at a lot of ropes. Bursts go out from the man who makes them. That one was going back to him."

"Yes."

Brom nodded slowly, and did not ask anything else. Cael was grateful. He had no answer ready that he would have trusted.

Lira came through the door between the rooms with her hair down and her boots in her hand. She looked at the arm, and said, "Plum," with great satisfaction, and sat on the crate desk because there was nowhere else to sit.

"I'm lighting the lamp tonight," she said. "In the north window. I said I would, the night after the floor, and it's the night after the floor." She put her boots on, one and then the other. "I'll not be there all night. Only an hour. I want to see if my feet still know where they live."

"And the woman?" said Brom.

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"Not now," said Lira. "Tonight. After the lamp. All three of us, at the table, with something hot." She stood up and stamped her heel down into the boot. "When we have it, I want my feet under me."

---

He walked the district that afternoon by himself, because he could not do anything else and could not sit still.

He went with no direction at all, which he had not done since the first month after he came down the hill, and he let his feet take him wherever they liked. The left arm was in the scarf and the right hung, and people looked at both and then at his face, and some of them nodded and some of them looked away quickly, the way people look away from a man they have been talking about. The news had gone round the hill in the night, and not only the news of the bout. The doorkeeper with the thick ear had heard a woman give the name of an academy at the door, and had told the eel-market men, and the eel-market men had told everybody they sold a bowl to. By noon the whole of the market row knew that a woman from Greyvane had sat on the stone with the boy after the floor cleared. Half of them had already decided what he would do.

Cael found that he knew the place.

It surprised him, how well. He had come down that hill a year and a half ago with a bag and a direction and nobody's name, and now he could not walk a street of it without his eyes sorting it the way they sorted a bench. The paper-stall man had moved his slate line again, this morning, without being asked; it now stood further out than anybody's in the row, and the man said, as Cael passed, "Feeling well?" in the voice of a man asking after the weather on a day he can see out of his own window. The pie woman's new boy had a black eye that was certainly not from a pie. The dried-fruit woman had put her prices up a copper on everything, because of the crowds. When she saw him she put them down again on the figs only, and only for him, and would not take his money for those either.

He knew, without anybody having told him, that the chestnut man on the corner had a daughter in the Ranked core who never came down the hill, and that the man watched the north gate on market days in case she did. He knew that the heavyset man's wife hid the good sugar in the flour bin, and that the sister knew, and that each of them thought the other did not know. He knew which of the dock partner's knees had gone and in what year, and that the four girls from the wall had a fifth friend who had stopped coming when her father found out where she went in the evenings. He knew that the betting man had a wife who did not know he was a betting man and believed he sold rope. None of it was in any book. Not one word of it was in any book of his. He knew it the way you know the stairs of your own house in the dark.

The mending-stall woman looked up from under her pennants, which had sold out, as he went by.

"You're going," she said. It was not a question.

"Nobody's said I'm going."

"Nobody has to." She bit off a thread. "They'd better feed you, wherever it is. You were half the size you are now when you came down that hill. I mended the shirt you came in. I've still got the bit I cut off the cuff." She went back to her needle. "Go on. You're in my light."

The betting man was sitting on the step of the pump with his board across his knees and nothing written on it. He looked up at Cael for a long moment.

"Best night of my life," he said. "Worst night of my life. I'll not know which for a week." He shook his head. "Twenty years, and I never once saw both columns pay out on the same bout. You've broken the board, lad. I'll have to think of a new way to lose money." He looked at the empty board. "I suppose I'll forgive you. Somebody's got to."

At the corner by the Ironyard's side door, where the wall was low enough to sit on, a woman was waiting. He knew her: a Stone from the tannery lanes, broad and grey and slow-spoken, whom he had beaten once on a side floor in his first winter, in the fourth exchange, and who had not said a word to him in all the months since. She did not get up from the wall when he stopped.

"You'll tell them where you learned it," she said.

It was not a question either. Cael understood it the way he understood a line in Vell's book: a whole judgment, closed and witnessed, in the fewest words that would hold it.

"I will," he said.

She nodded once and looked away up the street, and that was the end of it. He walked on, and found that his eyes had gone hot, and let them.

His feet took him round by the north gate and back down the tannery lane. Somewhere along it he found himself thinking about the keepers, which was an odd thing to think of on a cold afternoon, and he let himself. Vell had a letter every month or so from the salt-end keeper, and one from a keeper at the coast whose courier had come twice that season. There was the lamp-oil keeper in Reydan's city, who had written down the river at Reydan's asking and lent Dace a book he wanted back. There were others whose names Cael had never heard, and Vell wrote to all of them in her small square hand. None of them had ever met. None of them had a guild, or a licence, or a hall. Between them they kept the only honest record of a few thousand fighters that existed anywhere in the world, and they kept it straight by writing to each other.

He had thought, when he came down the hill, that the Unranked District was where the Compact's ladder let people fall. He did not think that any more. Walking the tannery lane with his arm in a scarf, he thought it was where people went to build a ladder of their own, once they had found out that the other one had never been meant to hold them. And he had been given a rung on it. Not a rung handed down from anywhere; a rung he had been watched making, one bout at a time, by people who wrote down exactly what they saw.

If he went, that was what he would be walking out of.

At the top of the tannery lane he stopped, with the river's cold coming up the slope at him, and found there was nothing left to decide. Cael had decided it some time ago, without noticing, somewhere between the stone and the woman on the wall. All that was left to find out was the price of going.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-02-iron-circuit/manuscript/chapter-56.md

# Chapter 56 — I'm Going

The lamp was lit in the north window of the Ironyard at dusk, for the first time in a fortnight.

Cael saw it from the bottom of the market steps on his way back from the tannery lane. It was a small yellow square high up in the dark wall of the old foundry, and he stood in the cold and looked at it for some time before he went on down. All fortnight there had been no lamp there, because she had put it out on the third day so that every hour she had would be his. He had not known, until he saw it lit again, how much he had missed it. It had been there through the whole of the autumn. It had been the last thing he saw from his window at night and, often, the first thing in the morning. On the nights when the Log would not come right, or a bout would not leave his head, he had lain and watched that square of yellow across the roofs and known that somebody else on the hill was awake and working at a thing that would not come right either. It had not made the thing easier. It had made it less lonely, which was a different help, and in some ways a better one.

He did not go up. It was her hour. He went round instead to the alcove by the side passage, which was dark and cold and smelled of chalk and straw, and sat on the bench against the back wall with his arm in the scarf. Through the arch, across the empty main floor and up the north stair, he could hear her.

He could not see her. He did not need to. He had heard her at that lamp a hundred nights from his bed, through two streets and a wall, the sound of a staff end on stone and feet on boards, and he knew the shapes of the sounds the way he knew the shapes of the words in Hesk's hand. Tonight they were slow. There was a step, and a long pause, and a step. A turn. The staff end touching down once, lightly, like somebody tapping a table to see whether it is sound. Then the same again, from the other side.

She was not training. She was asking her feet questions, one at a time, and waiting for them to answer.

After a while the pauses got shorter. Then there were no pauses at all, only a long unbroken run of sound from the north stair: step and turn and step, the staff end, the heel, the step that did not stop. He sat on the bench and listened to her feet remember where they lived. Cael found that he was smiling in the dark like an idiot, alone in an alcove with his arm in a scarf.

Brom came in at the side passage when the hour was nearly over. He had a covered pot in one hand, held out in front of him as if it might go off, and a loaf under the other arm.

"The cookshop," he said. "I told him who it was for. He gave me twice as much and wouldn't take the second copper." He sat down on the other end of the bench, carefully, with the pot on his knees. He listened for a moment to the sound from the north stair. "She's found it."

"She never lost it."

"No," Brom agreed. "But she was afraid she had." He was quiet a moment. "That's not the same, and it's not nothing either."

The sound on the stair stopped. They heard the lamp go out, and her feet come down the north stair and across the main floor in the dark, quick and sure. Then she was in the arch with her staff on her shoulder and her face pink from the cold and the work, and she looked at the two of them sitting on the bench like a pair of owls, and at the pot on Brom's knees.

"Good," said Lira. "Upstairs. My table. Now. Before I lose my nerve."

---

Her table was the landing table, in her room at the boarding house, the one with a short leg that she had wedged with a folded bill from the paper stall. They sat round it with the pot in the middle and the lamp beside it and the door to the landing propped open, because three people and a lamp in Lira's room was too warm for anybody with their coat on. The heavyset man's wife came up the stair while they were spooning out the stew and put a fourth bowl on the landing without a word, as if a fourth bowl were simply what happened now. She went down again.

It was a small room and it held a great deal of Lira. Her staff leaned in the corner by the window with its grip half mended. Her single iron pan hung from a nail by the door, scoured bright, because she cooked for four on the house's fire and would not let anybody else touch the pan. Her measuring stick lay along the top of the press, notched in pencil from the winter, when she had measured every one of his bursts against it and written the numbers in his book in her own hand. Over the bed, on a string, hung the wrist cloths she had worn through, washed and dried and kept, for no reason she had ever been able to give him.

Cael could not hold a spoon in either hand. Lira held it for him, as she had the night before, without any comment at all this time, between her own mouthfuls.

When the pot was half empty Cael began. He had planned what he would say, walking back from the tannery lane: the offer as Quenna had laid it out, one piece at a time, the two tracks and the monthly assessments, the file, the three in eleven years. He had meant to set it all out on the table between them like a page of the Log, so that each of them could look at all of it before anybody said a word.

He got as far as the first piece.

"I'm going," said Lira.

He stopped.

She had put the spoon down. She was not looking at him, and she was not looking at Brom. She was looking at the lamp.

"I'm going," she said again, as if she wanted to hear how it sounded the second time. It sounded the same. "I knew it on the stone last night, before she'd finished saying *Fenmark*. It was there this morning when I woke, sitting on the end of my bed like a cat. I lit the lamp tonight to make sure I wasn't only saying it because I was frightened, or because you'd go anyway and I didn't want to be left on this hill on my own." She turned her head and looked at him at last. "I'm not. I went up there and asked my feet. They said yes."

"I know," said Cael.

"I know you know. You knew before I said it."

"I knew before you lit the lamp."

That made her laugh, a short surprised sound, and then she stopped laughing and was serious again. It was the face she wore at the south mark, before a bout.

"There's a thing I want to say about why," she said, "and I want to say it properly, so let me get to the end of it before either of you does anything with it." She put her hands flat on the table on either side of her bowl. "It's not the ceiling. Or it is, but not the way it was. You both know about the ceiling. A Wind on a Copper card with *expelled* on the bottom of her paper can't get a guild to sponsor her, and without a guild she can't get to an Arbiter, and without an Arbiter she can't move. I've walked to their gate four times this year and been turned round at it four times, and I've stopped walking. That's all true and it's all still there. But that isn't why."

Brom had stopped eating. Cael had not been eating anyway.

"It's Maud," said Lira.

She said the name plainly, the way Vell would have said it.

"I lost to her. Fifth exchange. You both saw it. And Vell wrote *provisional* and kept it there, because I hadn't fixed the middle yet, and she was right." She turned one hand over on the table and looked at the back of it, where the bars Maud's staff had left had faded to a faint yellow. "And afterward, for weeks, I kept thinking: nobody here is going to make me prove it again. Vell will book me when she thinks I'm ready. Dace will put me on a card. Half the hill will come and shout for me because they like me, and the four girls will cry whichever way it goes, and if I win, the line in the book will move up a rung, and that'll be that. Proved once. Kept forever." She looked up. "This hill is the kindest place I've ever been. It's kinder than my own mother's kitchen. And it's never once asked me the same question twice."

"Quenna will," said Cael.

"Quenna will. Every month. Somebody who doesn't know me and doesn't love me and doesn't care which girls are crying will stand at a chalk line and ask me whether I'm as good as I was last month, and better." She took a breath. "I want that. I didn't know I wanted it until she said *monthly* as if it were a stone she was handing me to carry. I thought: *yes, give it here.* I've been proving it to people who already believed me for a year. I want a stranger with a slate making me earn it again, every month, from nothing."

Nobody said anything for a moment. The lamp hissed. Down in the house, the heavyset man's chair creaked by the front window.

"That's the end of it," said Lira. "You can do things with it now."

"I'm not going to do anything with it," said Cael. "It's right."

"I know it's right. I wanted you to hear it before you started telling me about the re-certification track as if I hadn't been sitting on the stone when she said it." She picked up the spoon again, and filled it, and held it out to him. "Eat. You've gone white again."

---

Brom took a great deal longer.

He did not say *yes* and he did not say *no*. He ate the rest of his stew, slowly, all of it, and wiped the bowl with a piece of the loaf, and put the bowl down, and then he began to ask questions.

He asked them one at a time, in his flat careful voice, and he waited for each answer before he asked the next. What did Greyvane teach about Iron Skin, if anything? Was there anybody on the staff who read, or who had ever stood in front of somebody who read? How long did a standard student stay? Who paid, and how much, and was there work in the town for a man who had to earn it? Could a Copper with a clean line and no complaints against him simply walk to the gate and ask to be taken, the same as anybody else? What did such a man have to show, and to whom?

Cael answered the ones he could, which were few. Lira answered one or two more, from Fenmark. At Fenmark, she said, a standard student came in at fourteen or fifteen and stayed four years, if he was not sent home; the town round an academy always had work for anybody with a strong back and no pride, carrying and kitchens and the stables; and every academy she had ever heard of kept somebody on its staff whose whole business was reading. "They'd not have a reader like you," she said to Brom. "Nobody has a reader like you. But they'll have somebody who'll know what you are when you stand in front of him."

Brom nodded at that, and filed it, and went on to the next.

Then Brom asked the last one. Cael understood, from the way he asked it, that it was the only one he had really wanted to ask all along.

"Does the form ask for your family?"

Cael looked at him.

"Not the name," said Brom. "Anybody can have a name. Whether they want the house. Whether they want a letter from your father, or your father's friend, or the station that did your reading, saying who you are and what you're for." He had his big hands flat on the table, one on each side of the empty bowl, the way Lira had held hers. "Because if they do, I'll not go."

"You've got a real classification," said Cael slowly. "Iron Skin. Copper. From an Arbiter. You don't need any provision."

"I know I don't. That's what frightens me." Brom said it without any heat at all. "The provision's for people the paper can't hold. The paper holds me fine. It holds me in exactly the place my family's letters put me, with a man's note in the margin saying I was taught wrong from the start and ought to be read again at a better station." He turned his head toward the window, where the cold was coming in round the frame. "I got up from that table at fourteen because I wasn't going to be measured by what was written down about me in a room I'd been sent out of. I'll not walk into a new room and hand them the same paper and let them measure me by it all over again. Not even if the paper says something kind."

"So what do you want?"

"I want to go in on what my hands can do," said Brom. "On my line in Vell's book, and on my hands, and on whatever they put in front of me at their gate. The same as anybody. If they want Velmere, they can have it without me."

Lira was watching him with an expression Cael had seen on her face only once or twice before, at the south rope, when somebody she had bet on in her own head did something she had not seen coming.

"Ask her," she said to Cael. "Tomorrow. Ask her every one of those questions, the way he asked them."

"I'll write them down," said Cael. Then he looked at his hands and said, "You'll write them down."

---

Quenna's inn stood at the top of the market row, where the row turned into the road to the north gate: a tall narrow house with a bow window, which the carters used when they had money and the carters' inn when they did not. She was in the front room at a small table by the window, with a pot of tea and her small book open beside it. She did not seem surprised to see him come in with his arm in a scarf and a folded page held out in his left fingers.

"Sit down," she said. "Before you fall down. Everybody in this town seems to say that to you."

He sat. The front room of the inn was the kind of room a carter with money would think of as fine: a carpet worn through to the threads in a path from the door to the hearth, a clock on the mantel that ran a quarter of an hour fast and that everybody in the house allowed for, and a bow window full of the market row going about its morning, barrows and baskets and breath going up in the cold. Quenna had her back to the room and her face to the window, so that she could see the whole row without being seen from it, and Cael noticed that, and filed it, and then gave her the page. Lira had written Brom's questions out in her own fast slanting hand, every one, in the order he had asked them, and at the bottom, underlined, *DOES THE FORM ASK FOR HIS FAMILY*.

Quenna read the page through once, and then again, more slowly. Then she laid it flat on the table and put her cup on the corner of it, as if to keep it from blowing away.

"Brom," she said. "Iron Skin. Velmere."

"You know."

"I know his line. It's on the wall of your Circuit Master's building and in your keeper's book, for anybody who stands there long enough, and I have stood there six nights." She turned the cup a quarter-turn on the page. "Eight bouts on the main floor, seven won. The one he lost, his first. His win over you. A season of side floors before that, up the river, which I wrote to ask about. And his name in a stable book at Velmere, which I did not write to ask about, because I didn't need to."

"Then why didn't you speak to him? Last night?"

Quenna looked at him over the cup for a long moment. It was the first time he had seen her decide whether to say something, and he filed that.

"Because the doors I hold are for people who can't get through the ordinary one," she said at last. "He can. He is a Copper on a clean card with a better line than half the second-years I teach. If I had turned round on that floor and offered him a door, I would have been carrying him through it. I watched him for two nights before I ever watched you. I did not think he was a man who would thank me for being carried anywhere."

Cael said nothing. He was thinking that she was right, and that she had seen it in two nights from the back of a hall, and that it had taken him most of a season.

"Tell him this," said Quenna. "Every one of these questions has an answer, and I'll write them out for him if he wants them. But the last one is the only one that matters, so here it is. The standard enrollment asks for what a man can show. It asks for his record, attested by somebody who saw it, and it asks him to stand at the gate in front of two of the staff and show them what he is. That's all. It does not ask for his house. It does not ask for letters from his father. It does not ask for the station that read him or the man who wrote in his margin." She tapped the page with one finger, under the underlined line. "If any letter comes to Greyvane from Velmere, with or without him, it will go in a drawer. I will see that it does."

"He'll want to come on his own."

"Then he'll come on his own." She took her cup off the page and pushed the page back across the table to him. "Tell him to bring his keeper's word for his line. That's all he needs. The rest he can show us with his hands."

---

He found Brom in the alcove, on the bench, with his elbows on his knees, not doing anything. Cael had never once seen Brom not doing anything. Even sitting still, Brom was always doing something: reading a room, or a man, or the grain of a loaf of bread. Now he sat with his big hands hanging between his knees and looked at the stone between his boots like a man in a waiting room who has been told the doctor will be some time. Lira was sitting on the floor against the straw post with her staff across her lap, mending a split in the grip with a strip of leather and pretending not to wait.

Cael stood in the arch and told him. He told him plainly, every word of it, in the order Quenna had said it, the way Brom liked things told: the record and the keeper's word and the two of the staff at the gate, and no house, and no letters, and a drawer for anything that came from Velmere. He told it bare.

Brom listened to all of it without moving.

When it was finished he sat for a while longer, looking at the chalk cross on the floor where Lira had dropped Cael four hundred times. Cael could see him going back through it, slowly, from the front.

"Then I'll walk in at their gate like anybody else," said Brom. "On my hands."

He said it to the chalk cross. Then he lifted his head and looked at Cael, and Cael saw that whatever had been loose in his face all evening had been fastened down, like a hatch before weather.

"You're not done being interesting, and I haven't solved the problem yet."

"The problem being me."

"The problem being you."

Lira snorted into her mending.

Brom did not smile. He looked down at his own hands, and turned them over, and looked at the backs of them. When he spoke again it was more quietly, in the voice he had used at the market wall the first time he ever told Cael anything true.

"And there's the other thing," he said. "I'll say it once, and then I'll not say it again, because it's not the kind of thing I say." He turned his hands back. "I had a home once, with a long table in it and good people round it, and it was the one I was supposed to have, and I got up from it. And then I came down a river to a town that didn't know my name, and sat on a bench beside a boy everybody said wasn't possible, and a girl with a staff hit me in the legs at dawn for my own good. And somewhere in that I found I had another one. One I picked." He looked at the arch, where the light from the main floor came in. "It's not this building. It's not this hill. I'll not let it go because it's moving three days east."

Nobody said anything.

Lira put down her mending and got up off the floor. She went to the bench and stood in front of Brom, and then, with great seriousness, she reached out and pulled one of his ears, hard, the way the sister pulled the ear of the dog that slept under the stairs.

Brom went red from the collar to the hair.

"That's for making me cry in an alcove," said Lira, who was not crying, quite. She sat down beside him on the bench and leaned against his arm. "Twice in two days. I'll not have it."

Cael stood in the arch and looked at the two of them on the bench, the big one red to the ears and the small one leaning on him with her staff across her knees, and found that he did not need to write anything down at all.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-02-iron-circuit/manuscript/chapter-57.md

# Chapter 57 — Find More of Them

He had written to Hesk on the Wednesday night, or rather Lira had, because he could not hold a pen for more than a few words at a time and a letter to Hesk was not a thing he would do in a few words at a time.

She sat at the crate desk in his room with the lamp at her elbow and his good paper in front of her, and he sat on the bed with his back against the wall and his arm in the scarf, and he told it to her. He told it the way he told things to Hesk, in order, without dressing: the fortnight and the bout, the forearm and the stone and getting up, the three reads, the mill wheel, the shoulder. Lira wrote it all down in her fast slanting hand. Twice she stopped and looked at him over her shoulder and said, "You want me to write *that*?" and he said yes. Then he told her the rest: the woman with the book, and the name she gave on the stone, and the two doors, and Lira's yes, and that Brom was asking his questions.

Then he stopped, and she waited with the pen lifted.

"Put," he said, "*I want to go. I've wanted to since she said it. But I'll not put my name to anything till I've heard from you.*"

Lira wrote it. Then she turned round on the crate and looked at him.

"Is that for him or for you?"

He thought about it, honestly.

"For him," he said. "He sent me down a hill with a bag and a direction and the money he'd put by, and he's never once asked me to come back up it. Every letter he's written me says *come home when you can*, and none of them says *come home*. I'm not going to go three days further from him without telling him first, and hearing what he says back." He looked at the window. "He'll say go. I know he'll say go. But I want to have heard him say it."

Lira looked at him a while longer. Then she turned back to the desk and wrote a last line he had not told her to write, and showed it to him: *L. wrote this. His hands are no good. He's eating. — L.*

He let it stand. He signed the bottom with his left hand, a scrawl that looked like a dropped piece of string, and she folded it and sealed it, and in the morning, on his way up the market row with Brom's questions, he put it in the post at the paper stall for the up-country coach.

On the Friday, the third day, he went back up the row to the inn with the bow window.

Quenna was at the same small table with the same pot of tea, as if she had not moved since Thursday. Perhaps she had not. He told her that Lira would take the re-certification track, and that Brom would come to the gate on the standard road, on his own feet, with Vell's word for his line. And then he told her that he would come too, and that he would not sign anything until he had a letter from his grandfather.

He had thought she might be impatient. She was not. She looked at him over the cup, as she had the day before, and he watched her decide something again.

"How far is he?"

"Denvash. Days, by the coach. Two each way, three if the roads are bad."

"Then I'll wait for your grandfather," said Quenna. She said it the way somebody else might have said *pass the salt*. "I'd thought to take the coach on Monday. I'll take a later one." She turned a page of her small book and wrote something short in it; he could not see what. "Most of the people I've sat across this table from wanted me to tell them what to do. It's a rest to meet one who already knows and wants to hear it from the right person first."

---

The Ironyard on the Friday afternoon was a different building from the one he had stood in on Tuesday night.

The lamps were down off the beams, all but the fourteen ordinary ones. Somebody had swept the stew corner so hard that the stone there was paler than the rest of the floor. The pennants were gone from the north rail, though one had been left hanging over the side door, by accident or not, a small red triangle turning slowly in the draught. A side-floor card was going, two Coppers he did not know, and forty people watched them as if nothing had ever happened in this room larger than a side-floor card.

Dace was at his wall.

He did not turn round when Cael came up beside him. He had a piece of white chalk in his hand and was doing something to the bottom of the next week's card, and he went on doing it.

"She's from Greyvane," said Dace, to the slate.

"You knew?"

"I knew Tuesday night at the door. She showed me her pass properly, finally, after I'd honoured it on faith for a month. She said she thought I'd earned that." He wrote a name, rubbed it out with the side of his hand, and wrote it again an inch lower. "I've known since Wednesday morning you were going. So has everybody on this hill with a pair of ears. The doorkeeper's told the eel men, and the eel men have told the river." He blew the chalk off his fingers. "When?"

"I don't know yet. After I hear from Hesk." Cael looked at the bottom of the card, where Dace's hand had been. "There's a thing on Tuesday."

There was. He had seen it on the slate two weeks ago and forgotten it, the way you forget a thing you agreed to before your life turned over. On the coming Tuesday, in the second slot of the main-floor card, in Dace's white chalk: *Ulric — Cael.* Ulric had come to Dace a month before Reydan arrived on the hill and asked for it. He was the Blade Cael had beaten in the fifth exchange months ago, before Brom, before any of it, the one who had wanted to know, afterward, what Cael had been watching. Dace had put it up, and there it had stayed, through all of it, two weeks of preparation and a bout the whole river was talking about, small and white and patient at the bottom of the slate.

"I can wipe it," said Dace. "Nobody'd blame you. Ulric wouldn't. He was at the north rope on Tuesday; he'll have seen your arm." He held up the chalk rag. "One pass of this and it's gone, and nobody on this hill would think the worse of you for a minute."

"I said I'd fight him."

"You did."

"Then leave it."

Dace lowered the rag. He looked at Cael for a long moment, sideways, the way he looked at a name on the wall when he was deciding where to move it.

"You'll not be able to hit anybody by Tuesday," he said. "Not properly. Not with either hand."

"I'll not need to hit him properly." Cael looked at the floor, at the rope where Reydan had walked him like a beast to a pen. "I know how this floor ends a bout without anybody hitting anybody. I learned it on Tuesday from the other side."

Dace was quiet. Then the corner of his mouth went in, a very small amount, the most Cael had ever seen it do.

"The rope," he said.

"The rope."

"Ulric'll hate that."

"Ulric wanted to know what I watch. I'll show him."

Dace turned back to the wall. He did not touch the bottom of the card. Instead, beside *Ulric — Cael*, very small, so that nobody but the two of them would ever notice, he drew the mark he had drawn once before beside Cael's name, at the start of everything, when he had first said *watch this one*. Then he rubbed it out again with his thumb, and laughed under his breath, a sound Cael had never heard him make.

"No," said Dace. "I've watched it. Go away. You're blocking my light."

---

Hesk's letter came on the Sunday, after chapel, in the second post.

The heavyset man brought it up himself, which he had never done before. Letters were left on the stair for whoever they belonged to; that was the rule of the house. But this one he carried up and knocked at Cael's door with, and held out, and when Cael took it with the fingers of his left hand the heavyset man looked at the hand, and at the writing on the front, and then at Cael's face.

"Denvash," he said. "That'll be the old man."

"Yes."

The heavyset man nodded his ordinary amount and went down again.

It was a small letter. Hesk's letters were never large; he wrote them on the backs of mill drawings and old bills, folded small, in a hand like a row of nails. But this one was smaller than any Cael had had from him. When he got it open on the crate desk, slowly, with one hand and his teeth, it was a single half-sheet, and on the half-sheet there were three sentences and nothing else. No *Mind the hip*. No *Eat what the girl doesn't*. No mill wheel, no grey dog, no fixed joint. Only this, in the middle of the paper, with a great deal of white round it, as if Hesk had wanted the words to have room:

*Go. You need people who have seen you clearly and haven't left. Find more of them.*

Below it, a single *H.*

He read it, and then he read it again, and then he sat with it on the desk in front of him and did not read it at all. He only looked at where it sat on the paper, small, in all that white.

He knew exactly how it had been written. He could see it as if he had been standing at Hesk's elbow: the bench in the workshop cleared with one sweep of the arm, the lamp pulled close, Cael's long letter in Lira's hand read through once and then again. Then a long while sitting. Hesk had sat like that over a bearing that would not seat, Cael had seen him do it a hundred times, turning the problem over without touching it until he knew what was wrong. And then a clean sheet, and a pen, and three sentences, and nothing more, because nothing more was needed and Hesk had never in his life written a word that was not.

When Cael was ten, Hesk had set him to make his first joint: two lengths of beech to be fitted at a corner for a drawer. He had cut it badly and fitted it worse, and then, afraid of it coming apart, he had driven six pins through it, then a seventh, until the wood round the joint was more holes than beech. Hesk had picked it up and turned it over in his big hands and said nothing for a long time. Then he had pulled every pin out with the pincers, one at a time, and handed the pieces back. *Cut it again,* he had said. *Cut it till it holds by itself. Every pin you drive is a thing you're telling me you don't trust.*

There were no pins in this letter. Cael knew every fear the old man had: the Compact, and the long road, and strangers with good manners and paper in their hands. He had carried them since a certification office in Denvash wrote a word on a form, and he would carry them to his grave. Not one of them was on the half-sheet. Hesk had cut the joint and let it hold by itself.

*Find more of them.*

It was not advice. It was work, the kind Hesk gave him at the bench: a thing to be done, which was hard, and which the old man plainly did not doubt he could do.

The lamp burned down a finger's width while he sat there. Then he got up and went through the wall to show it to Lira. She read it standing up, in her stockinged feet, with her hair down, and gave it back to him without a word and went to the window and stood there with her back to him for a while.

"I'm one," she said, to the window. "Aren't I. Of the people."

"You're the first."

"Good," said Lira, and did not turn round, and he went back through the wall and let her be.

He answered it that evening. He wrote it himself, in his own left hand, because this one he would not have anybody write for him. It took him most of an hour, and the hand was terrible, and it was short.

*I've two already. They've both seen all of it and neither of them has left, and they're both coming with me. I'll look for more. I'll write from the gate. — C.*

He read it over once. It would do. Then, because Hesk would want to know and nobody else would ever tell him, he wrote underneath, smaller: *The arm's healing. I'm eating. L. holds the spoon.*

Brom came for supper, as he did most nights now, and the fourth bowl was on the landing waiting for him. Cael gave him Hesk's half-sheet across the table without saying what it was. Brom wiped his fingers and took it carefully by the edges, the way he took anything of Cael's, and read it from the front, slowly, though there were only three sentences. Then he read it again.

"He's a careful man," said Brom at last.

"He's the most careful man I know."

"Three sentences." Brom laid the sheet down on the table and squared it with one finger, so that its edge ran true with the table's edge. "My father wrote me a letter once that was nine pages long. It was about the reading, and what was to be done about it, and who would be written to, and when. Nine pages, and on every one of them somebody else's hands were on me." He took his finger off the sheet. "Your grandfather's put three sentences on half a page, and there's more in them than in all nine." He looked at Cael. "I'd like to meet him. I'd like to ask him what he left out."

"Everything he was afraid of."

Brom thought about that for a while, with his spoon in his hand.

"That's the hard part," he said at last. "Leaving it out." He began to eat. "I've never once managed it."

"You managed it on the floor," said Lira, from her side of the table. "You managed it on Tuesday at the north rope, for four exchanges, with your face. I watched you. You looked like a man at a stranger's funeral."

Brom went faintly red, and ate his stew, and did not answer, which Cael understood to mean that she was right.

---

He took his answer to Quenna on the Sunday afternoon, while the ink on Hesk's letter was still in his head like a bell that has not stopped ringing.

She had the papers ready. He had known she would. They lay on the small table by the bow window in a neat flat pile, and beside them a pen and an inkwell and a stick of sealing wax, and the pot of tea, which was fresh. She did not ask him whether he had heard from his grandfather. She looked at his face when he came in and poured him a cup.

There were four sheets. She went through them with him one at a time, slowly, turning each so that it faced him. The first was the academy's acceptance. The second was the provision itself, copied out, so that he could read it in its own words: the old sentence about children whose ability came before their reading, and then the other sentence, the one she had been carrying for years, *any practitioner without a standard Arbiter-issued classification*. The third was a form that asked for his Path and tier and rank, on which every box had been struck through with a single neat line and initialled in the margin, *Q.* The fourth was blank except for a line at the bottom and the words *demonstration-provision track* printed above it.

"That one's yours," said Quenna.

He signed it with his left hand. The signature looked like a dropped piece of string again, worse than the first, and he looked at it with something like shame. Quenna looked at it with no expression at all.

"It's legible," she said. "It's yours. It'll do." She took the sheet and blotted it and laid it on the pile. "That's the first time, I think, that any paper has called you anything at all and you've agreed with it."

Cael had not thought of that. He thought of it now, sitting at the small table with the tea going cold, and found that she was right. The certification office had written a word about him without asking. The registry had written another without asking. Vell's ledger had written *No Path designation*, and that was true, but it had been written about him and not by him. This was the first sheet in his life that named the road he was on, with his own mark at the bottom of it, however badly made.

"I'll take the coach on Tuesday," said Quenna, gathering the papers. "The gate will have your three names on it by the time you get there. Come within the fortnight, if you can, and before the first snow on the hill road, because the hill road is no place to be after the first snow." She paused with the papers in her hands. "And mind the shoulder on the way. I'd like to see what it does when it's mended."

He went down the market row afterward toward the boarding house, slowly, and stopped at the paper stall without quite meaning to.

The paper-stall man looked up from his slate.

"Feeling well?"

"Very."

"I heard." The man put down his chalk. "What'll it be? You've a line here as long as my arm, and I've a notion you'll not be standing at this stall much longer to run it down."

Cael looked along the shelf behind the man, past the ink and the pens and the good paper, to the place at the end where the man kept the things nobody on the hill ever bought. There were ledgers with marbled covers for shops that had not opened yet, and a writing slope with a cracked lid. And there was a binder of stiff brown board with three iron rings down its spine, the kind a clerk in the Ranked core would keep his copies in, so that pages could be added and taken out and moved about without anybody having to unstitch anything.

"That," said Cael.

The man took it down and blew the dust off it and set it on the counter.

"Nobody's looked at that in four years," he said. "What's a lad from this hill want with a binder?"

Cael thought of the Power Log on his crate desk. It had started as a slim stitched notebook split off from the observation book, two entries and a column he was adding that month. It had pages tipped in at the back now, and pages pinned in at the front, and a whole section, *Anomalies*, written on two leaves he had cut from the grey book and glued in with flour paste. The stitching had begun to give at the top. There was no more room in it. There had never been going to be enough room in it.

"Somewhere to put the pages," he said, "that I haven't written yet."

The man looked at him a moment longer. Then he wrote a figure on the slate, and rubbed it out, and wrote a smaller one, and then rubbed that out too and put nothing at all.

"Take it," he said. "Bring it back full."

That evening, at the landing table, Lira cut the stitches of the Power Log with her knife, very carefully, one at a time, while he held the spine down with his left hand. Then the two of them punched the old pages with a nail and a hammer and set them into the binder in order. First the front, with the notices copied out at the head of each entry, Wind and Pressure and Iron. Then the fields under each, and the revisions, and the redirect's page, and *Habit* and *Shape*, and *Carrying*. Then, at the back, *Anomalies*, with session nine on its leaf, and the half second on the leaf after it.

When it was done there was more empty space behind the last written page than there had been written pages in the whole of the old book. Cael sat and looked at it, at all that clean board-backed space waiting under the rings, and found that it did not frighten him in the least.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-02-iron-circuit/manuscript/chapter-58.md

# Chapter 58 — Wind Only

Quenna took the coach on the Tuesday morning, and by the Tuesday evening the hill had already begun to behave as if she had never been there.

That was the way of the place, Cael had learned. It took a thing in, and talked about it for three days at every stall in the market row, and then it put the thing away somewhere and went back to the price of eels. By the Monday the talk at the pump was all about a carter who had put his wagon into the river at the ford and come out with the horse and lost the wagon. By the Tuesday a few people still nodded to Cael in the street a little longer than they used to. Most did not.

Cael found he was grateful for it.

His body had spent the week mending in the order it chose, which was not the order he would have chosen. The knees came back first. On the Wednesday he had gone up the market steps one at a time with a hand on the rail. By the Saturday he went up them like anybody, and by the Monday he had forgotten, for most of a morning, that they had ever been locked. The hands came next. The stupid brightness went out of the knuckles a day at a time until he could close both fists on the Sunday, though not hard, and not without telling himself to. The left forearm went through every colour Lira had a word for, plum and then slate and then a green she called *pond*, and finished the week the yellow of an old bruise. It was sore to press and no longer hot.

The right shoulder was the slowest, and the strangest.

It did not hurt the way the left hurt, with that long bright groove from the top of the arm to the breastbone. It did not hurt very much at all. It was simply quiet. Each morning Brom came early, before Lira's hour, and stood in the doorway of Cael's room and said, "May I?" and Cael said, "You may." Brom laid his big hand flat on the joint and read it, a whole palm's slow listening, and each morning he said the same thing.

"Still quiet."

On the Monday he added, "Less quiet than it was." And on the Tuesday, the morning of the bout, he stood with his hand on the shoulder a long time and said, "It's like a room after people have gone out of it, and somebody's begun to sweep."

By then the arm would lift to the height of the shoulder and no higher. That was enough to guard with. It was not enough to strike with, and it was certainly not enough to put through a redirect, and Cael had no intention of asking it to.

He watched Ulric on the Monday.

He did it out of habit as much as anything, the way he would have watched anybody whose name was on a slate opposite his. He sat on the east bench with the grey book on his knee through two of Ulric's side-floor bouts, a Copper Shield in the afternoon and a young Wind from the salt end after supper. He had not watched him properly since the night he beat him, months ago, before Brom had ever come down the river. Ulric had been a Copper Blade with a heavy slash and a tell: the right shoulder dropped a finger's width before the big cut, every time. Cael had found it in the second of four watched bouts and beaten him with it in the fifth exchange, and Ulric had come up to him afterward, still holding his side, and said, *What were you watching?* And Cael had told him.

Ulric had fixed it.

Not all of it. You could not fix a thing that deep in a season; it had gone into him at the post too young. But the drop was smaller now, and it came later, and twice in the Shield bout it did not come at all. Cael watched him find his feet against the young Wind, who was quick and silly and kept trying to go round him. Ulric did not chase. He stood and let the boy come, and turned on the spot, and every turn kept him in the middle of the floor while the boy ran round the outside. He had learned something since the autumn. He had been watching people too.

*Shoulder: smaller, later, sometimes gone,* Cael wrote under the old mark. *Doesn't chase. Keeps the middle. Turns on the spot. He's been somewhere and come back better.* And then, because it was true: *Good.*

---

The Tuesday card was an ordinary Tuesday card, and that was the best thing about it.

There were fourteen lamps on the beams and no more. There was no stew in the corner and no pennant over the side door. The betting man had his trestle back, with his chalk and his two columns, and the columns were the ordinary columns, names and prices, and nobody was arguing at them. Perhaps three hundred people stood round the main floor. Three hundred was a good crowd for a Tuesday, and a week ago it would have seemed like a great many; tonight the building felt half empty and comfortable, like a kitchen after a wedding.

Vell stood at her table with the red-taped book. She looked across at him once as he came in, over her spectacles, and lifted her pen perhaps a finger's width off the page. That was all.

Ulric was already at the south mark when Cael stepped over the rope. He was a square, plain young man, with a blade's thick wrists and a face that had been broken once at the nose and set a little to one side. He was looking at Cael's arms: the left forearm, yellow from wrist to elbow; the right, which Cael was holding a little stiffly at his side. His face went careful.

He came across the stone before Vell called anything, which was not done, and stopped a stride off and spoke low.

"I'll not go for the arms," he said. "If that's what you're thinking."

"I wasn't."

"Then I'm telling you anyway." Ulric looked at the yellow forearm again, and then at Cael's face. "I asked for this a month ago because I wanted to know if I'd learned anything. I don't want to learn it off a man who can't lift his arms."

"I can lift them," said Cael. "To here." He showed him: the right arm to the shoulder and no further. "I'll not hit you hard tonight, Ulric. I can't. I'll show you what I watch instead. You asked me in the autumn."

Ulric looked at him for a long moment. Something went over his broken face that might have been a laugh he did not let out.

"All right," he said, and went back to his mark.

Vell's voice came across the floor.

"Ulric. Blade Path. Copper, on a guild card." The pen moved. "Cael. Iron-equivalent. No Path designation." It moved again. "The rule in this room is that nobody is killed on my floor. Both of you know it. Both of you say so."

"Yes," said Ulric.

"Yes," said Cael.

"Then it's a bout."

---

He had decided before he came in what he would use, and he used only that.

The knock stayed shut, because he did not need it and this was not a night for needing what he did not need. The giving face stayed asleep, because there was nothing in either hand to give with, and the shoulder he did not even think about. What he had was his eyes, and his feet, and the Wind, which had been the first thing he ever had, from Lira, a winter and a half ago in a frozen yard. He meant to spend the whole bout on those three and nothing else, and see how far they would carry him.

The first exchange he gave away. He stood off and let Ulric come, and Ulric came the way he had come at the young Wind, not chasing, taking the middle. His blade was a practice blade from Dace's locked rack, dulled at the edge and heavy at the end. He swung it in short honest cuts that wanted Cael to commit to a side, and Cael would not. He went on his feet, left and right, and once on the hip, left, half a body over, the old fan. He watched the shoulder. It dropped, small and late, before two of the five cuts. Before the other three it did not drop at all.

*Two in five,* he thought. *Then the cuts it doesn't come before are the light ones. He's fixed it on the light ones. The heavy one still has it.*

"End of the exchange," said Vell.

In the second, he watched the middle.

Ulric owned it. He stood in it and turned on the spot as he had against the young Wind, and every turn kept his blade between Cael and the centre of the floor. Cael moved round him, and Ulric turned, and Cael moved again, and Ulric turned again. It was very good. It was the thing Ulric had learned since the autumn, and he had learned it well, and Cael watched it the way he would have watched a new tool in Hesk's workshop, turning it over to see how it was made.

It was made with the feet. Ulric turned on the ball of his right foot and brought the left round after. Every time he did it, the left came round a fraction wide, so that after each turn he stood a hand's breadth further toward whichever side Cael had gone. He was keeping the middle by turning. But he was not quite turning on the spot. He was turning on a spot that walked.

Cael went left, round him, and Ulric turned and walked a hand's breadth left. Cael went left again. A hand's breadth. Again.

He did not hurry it. He went round Ulric five times in the second exchange, slowly, on his feet and once on the hip, and took one heavy cut on the yellow forearm when the shoulder dropped and he was a breath slow reading it. It was not a hard cut. It still hurt like fire. And each time round, the middle of the floor that Ulric owned walked a hand's breadth further toward the north rope.

"End of the exchange," said Vell.

Ulric went back to his mark breathing hard, and looked round at where he was, and frowned. He was not stupid. He had seen something, and could not yet see what.

In the third exchange, Cael finished it.

He went left round him, as before, and Ulric turned and walked his hand's breadth. Left again; the breadth again. Ulric was close to the north rope now, two strides off it, then a stride and a half, and he felt it. Cael saw him feel it in the set of his back. He stopped turning and came forward instead, to take the middle back by force. It was the right thing to do, and Cael had been waiting for it since the first exchange. The shoulder dropped, small and late, but it dropped. It was the heavy cut.

Cael went on the hip.

He went left and forward in the fan, half a body over, the oldest thing he had, and the heavy cut went through the place where he had been and pulled Ulric after it. In the lock, standing with his weight caught and his breath held for the two *ands*, Cael did nothing at all. He did not need to. He had only to stand there, at the one place on the floor where Ulric's next step, after a heavy cut that had found nothing, would have to go.

Ulric took the step. He took it backward, the way a man recovers from a cut that has missed, to make room for the next. And the back of his calf met the rope, and his weight was going the wrong way, and there was nothing behind the rope but the north bench and the dock partner's knees.

He went over it. Not hard. He went over it the way a man sits down on a wall he did not know was there, and sat on the stone on the far side, with his blade still in his hand, looking up at Cael over the rope with an expression of absolute astonishment.

Then he began to laugh.

Vell's pen moved.

"Cael. Win. Third exchange. Forfeit by the rope."

The room made a sound that was half a cheer and half a laugh, the sound a crowd makes when it has watched a thing it did not expect and liked it. Cael went to the rope and held out his left hand, and Ulric took it, and Cael pulled, gently, because the left would not pull hard. Ulric came up and over and stood on the stone, still laughing, rubbing the back of his calf.

"You walked me," said Ulric.

"You walked yourself. I only went round."

"The turn." Ulric looked down at his own feet as if they belonged to somebody else. "The left foot. It comes round wide."

"A hand's breadth. Every time."

Ulric stood for a moment and looked at his feet, and his broken face did several things one after another.

"That's what you watch," he said.

"That's what I watch."

"Then I'll go and watch it myself." He let go of Cael's hand. "Thank you," he said, and meant it, and went off along the rope toward the side door, still looking at his feet.

Cael stood on the stone and went through himself, out of habit, and found almost nothing to count: a thin hip line from two bursts, a sting along the forearm where the cut had caught it, and his breath already easy. Nothing else had been asked of anything.

It had been, he thought, the least he had ever spent to win a bout on that floor. He could not remember the last time he had fought anybody for the plain pleasure of watching a thing and seeing it work, with nothing riding on it but a line in Vell's book.

He did not tell anybody that it was his last bout on that stone, and he did not say it to himself either, not in words; he let it be a bout, an ordinary Tuesday bout with an ordinary line in Vell's book, and went to fetch his coat from the rail.

---

The notice came late, after the bout, while he was still awake at the desk.

He was at the crate desk with the binder open in front of him and the lamp turned low, writing Ulric's line in the back pages in a hand that was nearly his own again. *Ulric. Main floor. Won, third exchange. Forfeit by the rope. Wind: two, both left. Feet. Eyes. Nothing else.* He had just written *Nothing else* and was looking at it, thinking that it was a pleasant thing to be able to write, when the room went quiet round him in the way he knew.

It was not the lamp, or the street. It was the particular quiet that had come three times before, and never once when he was waiting for it. A held breath of the whole world, and then, in the middle of the quiet, in front of him and in no place at all, the words.

```
FRAGMENT ACQUIRED
[unnamed] — Compression-adjacent. Duration: sustained. Integration: partial.
Tier equivalent: unknown.
Note: force absorption component. Damage redirect, contact range.
```

He sat very still and read it.

He read it the way he read every one of them: twice, slowly, and then once more, field by field, the way Vell read a line before she wrote it. And then he sat back against the wall and let his breath out. He found that his hands, both of them, had gone flat on the desk on either side of the binder, as if to keep it from moving.

It was not like the others.

The other three had come as strangers. Wind had arrived after a winter of Lira's mornings, and he had not known what it was or how to call it, and had spent half a year learning. Pressure had arrived after a hand on his shoulder in a yard, and he had spent months finding out it had two faces. Iron had arrived after three mornings of Brom hitting him for science, and he had spent a whole autumn learning to knock. Each of them had been a door in a wall he had not known had doors. He had opened each one and found a room he did not know, and had to learn its size in the dark.

This one he knew.

He knew the room. He had stood in it for half a second on the main floor a week ago, with Reydan's burst gathered in his right shoulder and pointing home, and he had not known what it was, or where it had come from, or whether he would ever stand there again. Now it had a name, or the half of a name that the notices gave: *Compression-adjacent*. It had a field that said *force absorption*, which was the gathering, and a field that said *damage redirect*, which was the turn, and the sending home. It had *contact range*, which was the shoulder, the burst inside him, and no further. It was the face of somebody glimpsed once in a crowd and now standing in the doorway with their hat off, saying, *Yes. That was me.*

He put his hand on the right shoulder, the quiet one, and held it there.

*So that's what you are,* he thought. *That's what was in there.*

He did not think, *I took it.* He was careful not to think that, because it was not true. He had not taken anything. He had not reached for it on the floor; he had only gone where it was already pointing. And he had not reached for it since. It had come, as all of them had come, unasked, after close and long and costly work against one person. It was found in him afterward, the way you find a splinter in your hand at night and remember the fence. That was all he knew about how they came. It was all he let himself know.

He turned to the back of the binder, past *Habit* and *Shape* and *Carrying*, to the leaves he had glued in himself.

The *Anomalies* page had two entries on it now, on two leaves. The first was session nine, the oldest, in his ordinary hand. The second was the half second, from the bout night, in the bad hand of a boy writing with his left fingers out of a sling. He read the second through. Then he drew a line under it, a single clean line across the leaf, and wrote beneath it, small and exact:

*Closed. Compression-adjacent, incomplete expression preceding integration. See the notice.*

The column balanced. He felt it the way a clerk must feel a long sum come right at the bottom, a small, absurd, enormous satisfaction out of all proportion to the ink.

Then he turned back one leaf, to session nine.

He read it all again. The thirteenth burst, the gathering met before it was sent, the push come apart in Brom's hands, the seven tries after with nothing. Brom's *Tide-adjacent*, and its limits. The three possibilities. *Evidence insufficient. Filed as anomaly. Monitor.* And under it, at the foot, in the field he kept for what would not go anywhere else: *Session nine. Could not reproduce. Still don't know what that was.*

The new notice did not touch it. He held the two side by side in his mind, as he had on the bout night, and they were no closer now than they had been then. The notice said *force absorption*, and in session nine there had been no force to absorb, because the push had come apart before it was ever sent. The notice said *contact range*, and in session nine Brom's hands had arrived on his chest with nothing behind them. The notice said *damage redirect*, and in session nine nothing had been redirected and nothing had been damaged and nothing at all had been paid. Whatever session nine had been, it was not this.

He added nothing to it, not a word, and kept the pen away from it altogether, so that the page stood as it had stood since the night before the bout.

One anomaly. Still one.

Then he turned to the front of the binder, to the clean leaves behind the three old entries, and ruled a fourth.

Not with the six fields, though; he had been meaning, since the night he put the pages under the rings, to do the old entries over in a shorter way. The six had turned out, in a year of use, to be four questions, two of them asked twice: what it does, and where, and when, all in one; what it is good for; what it costs; how much of it comes when he calls. He ruled the new entry the short way, four heads, and meant to bring the other three over to match it on the road, where there would be time.

*Compression-adjacent. Reydan.*

*Function, as known once, in a bout: force already inside me, gathered instead of passed, turned, and sent back along the line it came in by. Contact range. The force has to be in the body first; nothing before that.*

*Benefit, guessed: a burst, or any committed strike, turned into return and position. Strongest against exactly the kind of blow I can least get out of the way of.*

*Cost, guessed: the shoulder it went through, all of it, for a week. Assume it's dear until I find out it isn't.*

*Integration: partial. Untried on purpose. Each of the others took weeks before it would come when asked. Expect the same. Begin small. Begin when the shoulder has something to give.*

Under it, outside the heads, smaller:

*Found in me afterward, the same as the others. Not taken. Not called. I went in on it once because it was already pointing, and that's all I did.*

He read it back. Then he turned to the very first leaf of the binder, where a year ago he had first counted what he had, one finger on each entry. He counted again, the same way, with a finger on each.

Wind. Pressure. Iron. Compression.

Four confirmed fragments. One anomaly.

He sat looking at that for a long time, while the lamp burned down.

---

He knocked on the wall. Not the other knock; the ordinary kind, with his knuckles, twice.

Lira came through the door between the rooms in her nightgown with a shawl over it, holding a candle, her hair in a plait over one shoulder and her face full of sleep and alarm.

"What," she said. "What is it. Is it the shoulder."

"It's the shoulder," said Cael. "In a way." He turned the binder round on the crate desk so that it faced her and held the lamp closer.

She read it standing. She read the notice through once, and then the closed line on the *Anomalies* leaf, which he showed her, and then the new entry, all four heads. She read slowly, the candle tilting in her hand so that wax ran down onto her fingers, and she did not seem to notice. When she had finished she stood holding the candle and looking at the page.

"The fourth exchange," she said.

"Yes."

"The thing that went back." She was quiet a moment. "I saw it from the rope. I didn't know what I was seeing. I thought you'd found something new in the redirect. Something you and Brom had kept back from me."

"We didn't keep it back. Neither of us knew."

"I know that now." She looked at him over the candle. "Four," she said.

"Four. And one."

She set the candle down on the desk, carefully, and put her waxy fingers flat on the open page beside the new entry, not on the writing, beside it, the way she had once put her hand in his book when it was a slim stitched thing with two entries in it, and said *It would be lonelier*.

"I'm going back to bed," she said. "You're going to bed too. And in the morning you'll go down to the carters' inn before Brom's up and tell him, because you tell him the same day, and it's after midnight, so it's already tomorrow, and if you don't tell him before breakfast he'll know from your face and be hurt that you didn't."

"I know."

"I know you know." She picked up the candle. At the door between the rooms she stopped. "It was in you all week," she said. "When I was holding the spoon. And neither of us knew."

"No."

"That's a strange thing to think about," said Lira, and went through, and he heard her bed take her weight on the other side of the wall.

He went down to the carters' inn at first light, through a frost so hard the cobbles rang, and Brom was already up, sitting on the bench outside the stable door with his breath going up in the cold, as if he had been expecting somebody. Cael gave him the binder open at the new page. Brom read it from the front, every word, the notice and the four heads and the line underneath, and then went back to the notice and read that again.

"*Contact range*," said Brom. "*Damage redirect*." He looked up. "It's mine. It's the thing I found in the boat shed. Hardened at the right instant, and the man went back along his own line."
